# workspace workflow

Run a workflow in a workspace: an ordered list of steps, each done by the agent in the workspace's Claude pane. `workspace step` is the agent's side of it.

## Usage

```sh
workspace workflow list [--json]
workspace workflow show [ID] [--source] [--json]
workspace workflow validate PATH|- [--id ID] [--json]
workspace workflow add ID PATH|- [--dry-run] [--json]
workspace workflow update ID PATH|- --if-sha256 SHA [--dry-run] [--json]
workspace workflow remove ID [--yes] [--dry-run] [--json]
workspace workflow install [--source N] [--json]
workspace workflow uninstall [--yes] [--dry-run] [--json]
workspace workflow run ID [--name WORKSPACE] [--input KEY=VALUE]... [--note TEXT] [--pane PANE] [--dry-run] [--json]
workspace workflow render ID STEP [--name WORKSPACE] [--input KEY=VALUE]... [--json]
workspace workflow check ID STEP [--artifacts DIR] [--name WORKSPACE] [--json]
workspace workflow simulate ID --script FILE [--input KEY=VALUE]... [--json]
workspace workflow start [ID] [REF | --prompt TEXT] [--branch BRANCH] [--slug] [--base REF] [--input KEY=VALUE]... [--note TEXT]
                         [--agent-kind KIND] [--[no-]headless] [--prompt-timeout DURATION] [--force] [--dry-run] [--json]
workspace workflow status [RUN | --name WORKSPACE | --project [NAME] | --all] [--table] [--json]
workspace workflow status --needs-me [--name WORKSPACE | --project [NAME]] [--json]
workspace workflow events RUN [--since N] [--types A,B] [--follow [--timeout DURATION]] [--json]
workspace workflow files RUN [--json]
workspace workflow trace RUN [--table] [--json]
workspace workflow attempts RUN [--attempt-of STEP] [--json]
workspace workflow audit RUN [--attempt-of STEP] [--json]
workspace workflow stats ID [--last DURATION] [--name WORKSPACE | --project [NAME]] [--json]
workspace workflow compare ID [--by definition | --by pack:NAME] [--last DURATION] [--name WORKSPACE | --project [NAME]] [--json]
workspace workflow sweep [RUN | --name WORKSPACE] [--dry-run] [--json]
workspace workflow sweep --install | --uninstall [--dry-run] [--json]
workspace workflow sweep --installed [--json]
workspace workflow recover [--dry-run] [--json]
workspace workflow resume RUN --accept-guard --note TEXT [--retry] [--pane PANE] [--extra-attempts N] [--json]
workspace workflow resume RUN --restart-step [--note TEXT] [--pane PANE] [--extra-attempts N] [--extra-tokens N] [--extra-time DURATION] [--json]
workspace workflow resume RUN [--from STEP] [--note TEXT] [--pane PANE] [--extra-attempts N] [--extra-tokens N] [--extra-time DURATION] [--extra-rounds N] [--json]
workspace workflow cancel RUN [--json]
workspace workflow pause RUN [--interrupt] [--reason TEXT] [--json]
workspace workflow unpause RUN [--json]
workspace workflow approve RUN [--step STEP [--attempt N]] [--note-sha256 HEX] [--note TEXT] [--json]
workspace workflow reject RUN --note TEXT [--step STEP [--attempt N]] [--to STEP] [--extra-attempts N] [--extra-tokens N] [--extra-time DURATION] [--json]

workspace step done [--status pass|fail] [--summary TEXT] [--code WORD] [--json]
workspace step status [--json]
```

| Subcommand | Description |
|------------|-------------|
| `workflow show` | List the definitions, or print one: its inputs and what each step does. A `.yml` entry that can't be a definition (a name that is not an id, a directory) is listed as `INVALID` with why |
| `workflow validate` | Check a definition's text, from a file or `-` for stdin, with the checks a definition on disk gets. Lists every problem at once and writes nothing; see [Validating a definition](#validating-a-definition) |
| `workflow add` | Save a definition, from a file or `-` for stdin, as `~/.config/workspace/workflows/ID.yml` after the checks `validate` runs. Refused with `workflow_exists` when that file is there; a preset of the same id is left alone and the copy shadows it. See [Adding a definition](#adding-a-definition) |
| `workflow update` | Replace your `ID.yml` with new text, from a file or `-` for stdin, only if the file still has the `--if-sha256` you read. A file that does not pass the checks can be replaced. Refused with `stale_workflow` when it changed, `builtin_workflow` for a preset with no file of yours. See [Updating a definition](#updating-a-definition) |
| `workflow remove` | Delete your `ID.yml` (a link itself, never its target). Asks first unless `--yes` or `--dry-run`; refused with `confirmation_required` under `--no-input`. A preset of the same id is in effect again. See [Removing a definition](#removing-a-definition) |
| `workflow run` | Start a run in a workspace (the current one, or `--name`). An unset `task` or `pr` is [filled from the branch](#filling-inputs-from-the-branch). `--dry-run` checks the inputs, the project's commands, the packs and the pane, and starts nothing. Run from the pane it would use, the run is made and [parked](#resuming-from-the-runs-own-pane) |
| `workflow check` | Run one step's `status:` command once, outside a run, in this checkout. Takes no lock and writes no run state; see [Trying a step's check](#trying-a-steps-check) |
| `workflow graph` | Draw a definition's steps, gates, reviews and loops as ASCII, Mermaid, Graphviz or JSON, and with `--run` where a run stands. Writes nothing; see [Drawing a workflow](#drawing-a-workflow) |
| `workflow render` | Print the instructions a step's agent would be sent for its first attempt, composed without a run. Writes nothing; see [Rendering a step's instructions](#rendering-a-steps-instructions) |
| `workflow start` | Make a workspace from a ticket, pull request, issue or branch `REF`, or from `--prompt TEXT`, wait for its coding agent and run the workflow in it. The `ID` may be left out when config names a default. `--dry-run` checks everything and makes nothing. `--ref REF` in place of `ID` picks the workflow from the ref's kind ([routing](#routing-a-ref-to-a-workflow)). See [Starting from a ticket or a prompt](#starting-from-a-ticket-or-a-prompt) and [Default workflows](#default-workflows) |
| `workflow status` | The runs still going in this workspace, another's (`--name`), a project's (`--project`) or every workspace's (`--all`), or one run (`RUN`). Each shows its step and, when it is not moving, [why](#where-a-run-is-stuck). See [Which runs `status` shows](#which-runs-status-shows). `--table` prints one row per run beside its workspace: [the status table](#the-status-table). `--needs-me` lists only the runs waiting for you: [what needs you](#what-needs-you) |
| `workflow events` | A run's history (going or finished), one line per event, oldest first. `--since N` gives only the events after line N; at most 1000 at a time. `--types A,B` keeps only those types; `--follow` keeps reading and `--timeout` bounds it. See [Reading a run's events](#reading-a-runs-events) |
| `workflow trace` | A run as a timeline, going or finished: every transition, its spans and where the time went (active, stopped, waiting by reason, tokens, loops). `--table` prints a grid, `--json` one document. Reads the run file and its events and writes nothing. See [Tracing a run](#tracing-a-run) |
| `workflow attempts` | What a run recorded about each attempt, going or finished: outcome, failure code, HEAD before and after, hashes, packs, outputs, check, agent, session, tokens and reasons. Reads the run file and writes nothing. See [Reading a run's attempts](#reading-a-runs-attempts) |
| `workflow audit` | Check the evidence copies a run kept against the hashes recorded for them, going or finished. Exits 1 when a copy changed, is gone or was replaced. Reads the run file and the evidence directory and writes nothing. With `--json`, `next_actions` says what to look at after a mismatch. See [Auditing a run's evidence](#auditing-a-runs-evidence) |
| `workflow stats` | How each step of a workflow went across its runs, going and finished, in every workspace unless `--name` or `--project` narrows it: attempts, passes, failures, median time, resumes and tokens per run. `--last DURATION` keeps the runs started in that window. Reads the run files and writes nothing. See [Step statistics](#step-statistics) |
| `workflow compare` | Sets a workflow's runs side by side, grouped by the definition they ran or by the text of one pack their agent was sent: runs, share that passed first time and resumes per run, always with a warning that it shows a direction and proves nothing. Reads the run files and writes nothing. See [Comparing runs](#comparing-runs) |
| `workflow files` | The files a run keeps: each attempt's prompt, check log and proof logs and each step's `produces` entries, with whether each exists and its size. Reads file metadata only; a link is reported, never followed. See [Reading a run's files](#reading-a-runs-files) |
| `workflow sweep` | One pass over the runs going (a paused run gets a `paused` row, outcome `nothing`, next action `workflow unpause`): record a pane or agent that is gone, start an agent daemon that is not running, decide a step whose agent ended its turn unseen, and apply a step's `on_timeout`. Needs no agent daemon. See [Sweeping stopped runs](#sweeping-stopped-runs) |
| `workflow recover` | After a reboot or the loss of the tmux server, restore each workspace that has a going run no person holds, restart a step that was mid-turn and wake a run that was between turns. `--dry-run` says what would happen and changes nothing. See [Recovering after a reboot](#recovering-after-a-reboot) |
| `workflow resume` | Get a run going again; `--from STEP` starts again at that step; `--accept-guard --note TEXT` accepts a guard stop. Refused with `--from` at a waiting gate from a pane a run is bound to. Run from the pane it would type into, it is [parked](#resuming-from-the-runs-own-pane) (outcome `parked`) and carried out after the agent ends its turn. `--restart-step` starts a crashed step again in a fresh conversation (see [Restarting a crashed step](#restarting-a-crashed-step)) |
| `workflow cancel` | End a run and release what it holds |
| `workflow pause` | Hold a run: nothing new starts until `workflow unpause` (the current turn keeps running). See [Pausing a run](#pausing-a-run) |
| `workflow unpause` | Let a paused run go on, doing what the pause held. See [Pausing a run](#pausing-a-run) |
| `workflow approve` | Pass a gate. Refused from a pane a run is bound to, and from a process under the run's own pane, so an agent doesn't approve its own plan by mistake. Records the gate's note when it names one. See [Passing a gate](#passing-a-gate) |
| `workflow reject` | Turn a gate down: the gated step, or an earlier one named with `--to`, runs again with the note in its instructions. Refused where `approve` is |
| `step done` | For the agent: record what it says about its step, then end the turn. Optional |
| `workflow simulate` | Play a workflow through the real engine with a scripted fake agent and print the transitions; nothing real runs. See [Simulating a workflow](#simulating-a-workflow) |
| `step status` | For the agent: which run, step and attempt its pane is on, and whether the files the step must leave exist |

| Option | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| `--name WORKSPACE` | `run`: the workspace to run in. `render`, `check`: the workspace whose packs and commands are read. `status`: only this workspace's runs still going |
| `--artifacts DIR` | `check`: an existing directory standing for the run's artifacts (`WORKSPACE_RUN_ARTIFACTS`); default an empty temporary one |
| `--script FILE` | `simulate`: the script of what the fake agent does and how each check comes out |
| `--input KEY=VALUE` | `run`, `start`, `render`, `simulate`: a value for one of the workflow's inputs (repeatable) |
| `--interrupt` | `pause`: also press the agent's interrupt keys in the run's pane while it works |
| `--reason TEXT` | `pause`: why, 1 to 4000 characters |
| `--note TEXT` | `run`, `start`: added to every step's instructions. `resume`, `approve`, `reject`: added to the next attempt's |
| `--pane PANE` | `run`, `resume`: the pane to run in (`%19` or `0.1`), instead of the workspace's Claude pane |
| `--from STEP` | `resume`: start again at this step |
| `--accept-guard` | `resume`: accept a guard stop, taking the checkout as it is now as the run's base (needs `--note`; see [Accepting a guard stop](#accepting-a-guard-stop)) |
| `--restart-step` | `resume`: end the step's unfinished attempt as `interrupted` and start the step again in a fresh conversation; not with `--from`, `--accept-guard`, `--retry` or `--extra-rounds` (`usage`) |
| `--retry` | `resume --accept-guard`: run the stopped step again instead of moving on |
| `--to STEP` | `reject`: the step to run again (the gated step, or one before it) |
| `--step STEP` | `approve`, `reject`: the gated step you saw (`current.step` in `status --json`); refused with `gate_not_waiting` unless the run waits at that step's gate |
| `--attempt N` | `approve`, `reject`: that step's attempt you saw (`current.attempt`); needs `--step`; refused with `gate_not_waiting` unless the run is on that attempt |
| `--note-sha256 HEX` | `approve`: the sha256 of the gate's note you read (`gate_file.sha256`), 64 lowercase hex digits; refused with `stale_revision` when the note changed |
| `--source` | `show ID`: print the file's text, with its sha256 and problems, valid or not; see [Reading a definition's file](#reading-a-definitions-file) |
| `--prompt TEXT` | `start`: the task as free text: the workflow's `task` input, and the branch's name when there is no `REF` |
| `--branch BRANCH` | `start`: the branch for a `--prompt` (default: the prompt's words). Refused with a `REF`, whose branch comes from the ref |
| `--slug` | `start`: add the ticket title's words to a JIRA or issue branch, as `start --slug` |
| `--base REF` | `start`: the branch or ref a new branch starts from |
| `--[no-]headless` | `start`: start the session in plain tmux, as `start --headless` |
| `--agent-kind KIND` | `start`: the coding agent the main agent pane runs |
| `--prompt-timeout DURATION` | `start`: how long to wait for the coding agent to be ready (60s by default) |
| `--force` | `start`: start although the workflow's `applies_to:` does not list the `REF`'s kind (otherwise refused with `workflow_not_for_ref`); the run starts with a warning |
| `--ref REF` | `start`: take the place of `ID` and `REF`: run the workflow `workflows.by_ref.<kind>` or `workflows.default` names for the ref's kind, else the one that fits; see [Routing a ref to a workflow](#routing-a-ref-to-a-workflow). Not with an `ID`, a `REF` or `--prompt` |
| `--since N` | `events`: only the events after line N of the run's history (a whole number, 0 or more; default 0) |
| `--id ID` | `validate`: check the text as this workflow id |
| `--project [NAME]` | `status`: every run of a project, going or finished. With no NAME, the project of the current workspace; the optional NAME takes the next bare word, so `status --project wr_x` looks for a project called `wr_x` |
| `--all` | `status`: every workspace's runs, going and finished |
| `--table` | `status`: one row per run, finished ones too at every scope, with its workspace's status and what still exists (see [the status table](#the-status-table)) |
| `--if-sha256 SHA` | `update`: the sha256 of the file's raw bytes, from `show ID --source --json` or `show --json` (the list); required |
| `--dry-run` | `run`, `start`: report what would start and start nothing. `add`, `update`: run every check and write no definition (the workflows directory and `.ID.yml.lock` may still be created) |
| `--status pass\|fail` | `step done`: `pass` (default) or `fail` |
| `--summary TEXT` | `step done`: one line about what was done, or what is left |
| `--code WORD` | `step done`: a word for why, kept as the failed step's `details.code` (see [Failure codes](#failure-codes)). Lowercase letters, digits and `_`, starting with a letter, at most 40 characters; anything else is a `usage` error |
| `--json` | Print one JSON document |

## How a run works

`workflow run` writes the run's file, binds the pane to the run (see [`binding`](README.binding.md)) and starts the first step. For each step, the runner:

1. takes the locks the step `uses:` for the run, in sorted order, in the same queue as [`lock acquire`](README.lock.md) and [`dev up`](README.dev.md). If one is busy, the run waits in the queue and nothing is typed;
2. writes the step's instructions to `.workflow/<run>/steps/<step>.<attempt>.prompt.md` in the checkout, built by the [instruction composer](README.instructions.md#workflow-steps): the default packs, the workflow's packs and `instructions:`, the step's packs and prompt, and what the runner knows about this attempt;
3. types one line into the pane, `Read <file> and follow it.` A step starts in a fresh conversation (the agent daemon types `/clear` first, as [`agent-run restart`](README.agent-run.md) does) unless it says `context: continue`. Neither line is typed over a draft: a Claude Code pane whose input box holds text is refused with `pane_busy` and `details.reason` `input_not_empty`, and the draft stays. The grey suggestion Claude Code shows in an empty box is not a draft.

A step is done when the agent's turn ends in the bound pane, every file the step `produces:` has been written during this attempt as a non-empty regular file (not a symlink; see [Produced files](#produced-files)), and its `status:` check passes. The check runs through `sh -c` in the checkout with three environment variables: `WORKSPACE_RUN_ID`, `WORKSPACE_RUN_ARTIFACTS` (the run's `.workflow/<run>` directory) and `WORKSPACE_RUN_BASE_SHA` (the checkout's HEAD when the run started; empty on an unborn branch or for a run made before it was recorded). They are environment variables, never placeholders rendered into the command, so no input text reaches the shell. A file an earlier attempt left (before a reject, a `resume --from` or a loop back) does not count until the step writes it again; its instructions say so. The workspace's agent daemon ([`agentd`](README.agentd.md)) sees the turn end and decides the step; `workflow run` starts the daemon if none is running. The daemon says when the turn that ended began (the prompt that started it), and the end of a turn that began before the step's attempt did decides nothing: if you were talking to the agent in the run's pane when a step was started (at a gate, say), the end of that conversation does not pass the step. The step's own turn's end does. The agent never has to report: `step done` only adds its own word, and `step done --status fail` fails the step at the turn's end without running the check.

- A step with `gate: approve` waits for `workflow approve` once it has passed. The run holds no lock while it waits. See [Passing a gate](#passing-a-gate).
- A step that fails (its check exits non-zero or runs past its time limit, or the agent reported `fail`) goes back to the step its `on_fail: {goto, max}` names, at most `max` times, and never once the run has made `max_attempts` attempts across all its steps, nor into a step that has used its own `max_attempts` (plus any `extra_attempts`), which stops the run with `details.limit: "step"` on the `failed_check` reason. The step it goes back to is told what failed, and, with `log_tail`, shown the end of the check's log. With `backoff` the retry starts after a wait instead of at once ([Retrying a failed step](#retrying-a-failed-step)). After that the run stops and waits for you.

`workflow resume` and `workflow reject` hold to the same budget: one that would start an attempt (a failed or undelivered step run again, `--from`, a retry's wait ended, a `--pane` resume of a running step, any reject) is refused with `attempts_spent` (`details`: `attempts`, `max_attempts`, `step`) once the run has made `max_attempts` attempts, and with `step_attempts_spent` (`details`: `run_id`, `step`, `attempts`, `max_attempts`, `needed`) once the step it would start an attempt of (`--from`, a reject's `--to`, the step a waiting retry goes to, else the current step) has made its own `max_attempts`; the run's cap is reported first when both are spent, and nothing changes. Pass `--extra-attempts N` (a whole number from 1 up) to add N to that run's allowance (and, for a step with its own `max_attempts`, to that step's allowance, kept as `extra_attempts` on the step in the run file; `attempts_extended` then carries `step`, `step_extra_attempts` and `step_max_attempts`) and go on; N must cover the larger of the two overruns (the attempt about to start has to fit), and the refusal names the least that does (`details.needed`, also in its message); the run file keeps it as `extra_attempts`, the event log records `attempts_extended`, the definition is not rewritten, and a later `on_fail` check counts against the raised total (`failed_check` `details.max_attempts` shows it). It also applies when the budget is not spent yet. `resume --restart-step` starts a new attempt, so it is held to the same allowance, budget and time limit. A resume parked from the run's own pane is checked when it is parked and again when it is carried out, but not when it is typed again after a busy pane (that attempt was already counted); the flag is applied once, on release. A resume that starts no attempt (it only reports `unchanged`, decides a running step from a turn that is over, looks at a check, moves a passed step on, or asks for a lock again) is never refused and ignores the flag, as do `tick` and `workflow sweep`; attempts the sweep typed again (`redelivered`) do not count. Runs stored before this check get the same refusal. `workflow status` marks every action that would start an attempt (a plain resume only for a step that a resume would run again; resume `--from`; a gate's reject) with the `extra_attempts` need while the run has spent its attempts, read from the run as it is now. The need is `{"name":"extra_attempts","flag":"--extra-attempts","min":N}`: pass at least `min`, the larger of the run's shortfall and the shortfall of the step that action would start an attempt of (the same number as `details.needed` of the refusal it would get); it also appears when only that step has spent its own cap.
- With a `budget:` the run also stops before an attempt it can no longer afford ([Token budgets](#token-budgets)).
- When the last step passes, the run is `completed`, its locks are released and its pane is unbound.
- A check still running when its run is cancelled, killed or moved to another step is stopped within a few seconds, so it doesn't go on using the test database beside the lock's next holder.

`.workflow/` is listed in the repository's `info/exclude` (the common dir's, for a linked worktree), so the checkout stays clean and `.gitignore` is not touched. Run state lives outside the checkout, in `~/.local/state/workspace/.workflows/` (`$XDG_STATE_HOME`): `runs/<run>.json` and `runs/<run>.events.jsonl` while a run is going, `archive/` once it has finished (the newest 200 are kept).

Killing a workspace ([`kill`](README.kill.md), [`finish`](README.finish.md), `projects kill`) cancels its runs. Stopping one leaves its runs as they are: `status` shows `pane_gone`, and `workflow resume` after a relaunch moves the run to the workspace's Claude pane. A run of a stopped workspace keeps the locks it holds and its place in a queue, and takes a lock that frees while nobody can start its step: its workspace's agent daemon is what moves it. `status` warns about such a run; the ways out are `workflow cancel`, relaunching the workspace and `workflow resume`, or `workspace lock clear NAME`. [`restore`](README.restore.md) carries a run's binding to the pane it recreates, so the run goes on there. [`doctor`](README.doctor.md#workflow-runs) lists the runs still going whose checkout is gone, run files that cannot be read, and run locks whose run is over, each with the command that clears it; it changes none of them.

## Filling inputs from the branch

`workflow run ID` fills the workflow's declared `task` and `pr` inputs from the branch of the checkout it runs in, when you did not give them. The checkout is the `--name` workspace's, else the workspace detected from the current directory.

- **Open pull request on the branch** (found with `gh pr view`): `pr` is its number and `task` is `#N: Title (url)`, the same text `workflow start` seeds. A fork pull request fills like any other, since `workflow run` does no fork check.
- **No pull request** (none, or closed or merged): `task` is `Branch NAME` and `pr` stays unset.
- **`gh` is optional.** If it is missing, times out or fails, the run is not refused; `task` falls back to `Branch NAME`. `gh` is not called when you give both inputs yourself.
- **Nothing is filled** on the default branch, on the main checkout's branch, on a detached HEAD or during a rebase, so `input_required` still fires. In a plain clone with no linked worktree the checkout is the main checkout, so nothing is filled there either.
- **`--input` always wins**, and `--input task=` counts as given. Only inputs the workflow declares are filled. A filled value must still match the input's `pattern:`, or the run is refused with `invalid_input`.
- The dry run and the real run work out the same inputs.

Every row of `workflow run` (dry run, started, parked) has `inputs_from`: `{"task": "pull_request" | "branch", "pr": "pull_request"}`, or `{}` when nothing was filled. The dry run's `message` is the printed line and ends with, for example, ` Fills task and pr from pull request #12.` or ` Fills task from branch fix-x.`. `workflow show --json` marks each declared input that can be filled this way with `"fills_from_branch": true`. `workflow start` and `go` do not fill: they seed from their own ref.

## Which runs `status` shows

**Run history.** There is no separate history command. `status --project`, `--all` and `--table` include finished runs, and `status RUN`, `events RUN` and `files RUN` read a run after it has been archived.

**Across workspaces.** `workflow status --needs-me` lists the runs waiting for you; `workspace goal list` shows each workspace's goal in one line, with `stuck` for an idle, timed-out or pane-gone run (see [goal](README.goal.md)).

`workflow status` takes one scope. At most one of `RUN`, `--name`, `--project` and `--all` may be given; two of them are a `usage` error, and so is a scope flag with a `RUN`, which used to be ignored. An empty value (`--name ""`, `--project=""`) counts as not given.

| You give | You get |
|----------|---------|
| nothing | The runs still going in the current workspace. Outside any workspace: `not_in_workspace`, exit 1, naming `--name`, `--project NAME` and `--all` |
| `--name WS` | The runs still going in `WS`. A name no workspace has is not an error: it prints no runs, exit 0, because run files outlive their workspace |
| `--project` | Every run, going or finished, of the project that holds the current workspace. A workspace that is in no project (detected, but with no config) is `unknown_workspace`; outside any workspace it is `not_in_workspace` |
| `--project NAME` | The same for the project `NAME`, from anywhere. An unknown project is `unknown_workspace` |
| `--all` | Every run of every workspace, going and finished |
| `RUN` | That one run, going or finished |

**The current workspace** is the one the working directory belongs to, found in three steps: a `.workspace` marker, then a key in the state file, then the configured checkout (main or worktree) that contains the directory, the deepest one if several do. The last step means a linked worktree with no config of its own, inside a main checkout that has one, resolves to the main workspace.

**The current workflow** is the runs still going in the workspace, oldest first. A workspace whose runs have all finished has none: `runs` is empty and the exit code is 0, and the text output says `Finished runs show with --project or --all. Runs waiting for you show with --needs-me.` (The `--name` form prints no such hint.) A run's end is read with `--project`, `--all` or `workflow status RUN`.

**The header.** The text output of `workflow status` (no `RUN`) opens with one line of counts when any run shows: `3 going, 2 waiting you (oldest 2h 14m)`. `going` counts the runs shown that have not finished; `waiting you` counts the ones `--needs-me` would list, and `oldest` is the longest wait among them. With none waiting the line is only `N going`. The header is not printed for `workflow status RUN`, `--table`, `--needs-me` or `--json`; the counts are there to read at a glance, and a script counts the `runs` itself.

A project's runs are those whose workspace is one of its members, whose stored `project` is its name, or whose workspace is a worktree named `PROJECT.worktree-...`. These last two apply to going runs as well as finished ones, and they keep the runs of a worktree that was killed. So `--project` can list a same-named project's runs in any state.

Limits:

- Two projects with the same name share the runs matched by name.
- A run left under a mixed-case workspace name by a rename to lowercase is not matched by `--name` or the project's members, as before.

**Replacing the old forms.** Bare `workflow status` used to list the active runs of every workspace, and `--name WS --all` listed one workspace's runs of any state. `--name WS --all` is now `usage`. With `jq`:

```sh
workspace workflow status --all --json | jq '.runs[] | select(.state != "completed" and .state != "failed" and .state != "cancelled")'
workspace workflow status --all --json | jq '.runs[] | select(.workspace == "WS")'
```

An agent that needs a run's inputs reads them with `workflow status RUN --json`: a bare read can return two runs.

### What needs you

`workflow status --needs-me` answers "what is waiting for me?" across every workspace. It is a filter over the reasons and `actions` that `workflow status` already works out, so it reads the same panes and questions and no more: one daemon read for each workspace, however many runs it has, so a workspace whose daemon is dead costs its one-second wait once. A stopped review round (a `review_*` reason) and a review pane's permission prompt are listed too, with the run's own row naming the review.

```
RUN            WORKSPACE     STEP       WAITING  WHY                  DO
wr_2611...ab   api.fix-auth  plan       2h 14m   gate                 workflow approve wr_2611...ab --step plan --attempt 2
wr_2611...cd   web.search    verify     38m      failed_check (test)  workflow resume wr_2611...cd
wr_2611...ef   workspace     implement  21m      stalled (agent_gone) workflow resume wr_2611...ef
```

**Scope.** With no flag it shows the runs still going in every workspace, never finished ones (a bare `status` is this workspace; `--all` includes finished runs, so `--needs-me --all` is a `usage` error, as are `RUN` and `--table`). `--name WS` or `--project [NAME]` narrows it, with the same rules as `status`; the two together are a `usage` error. Finished runs are left out even under `--project`.

**Which runs count.** Every run still going with a shown reason is listed, so a reason added later appears until it is named as one that clears by itself. Left out: `turn_ended_background` (a hold for background tasks), a `retry_backoff` whose resume names no other pane (the wait ends alone), and a `waiting_lock` for 30 minutes or less, or with no `since` (nothing says how long it has waited); a lock wait past 30 minutes is listed, because a holder that has not let go in that time is stuck (its next command is the first of the reason's actions, `workflow status HOLDER_RUN`, `dev down` or `lock status RESOURCE --name WS`; never a bare `workflow cancel`, so `next_action` is null and `DO` is `-` when nothing else is offered). A run with no reason is listed when it is `stuck`: the sweep noted a stall (`step_stalled` with `agent_gone` or `daemon_down`, say) that nothing has put right and its pane is not working (a pane that cannot be read leaves the stall listed), or its pane is idle and the daemon gave the turn's end (the flag `idle`). A stale stall mark on a working pane, a `checking` step, a line still on its way or a hold for background tasks is not stuck. A working run and a [parked](#resuming-from-the-runs-own-pane) one are not listed. A [paused](#pausing-a-run) run is listed (`WHY` `paused`, `DO` `workflow unpause`) but gets no inbox row.

**In the inbox.** `inbox list --include-derived` shows a row for each run of this list that is waiting on a failed check, a lock wait past the 30 minutes, or a stall (`workflow_failed_check`, `workflow_waiting_lock`, `workflow_stalled`, `workflow_idle`), through the same rule, so the two lists agree; a gate, an undelivered step, a prompt or a question keeps the row it had. A step whose review round stopped (`review_*`), or whose review pane waits on a permission prompt, has a `workflow_waiting_you` row of its own, since review panes get no pane row. See [derived items](README.inbox.md#derived-items).

**Columns.** `WAITING` is how long since the reason's `since` (a stall's recorded time, or the turn's end for `idle`), `-` when the run has none. `WHY` is the reason's code, or the kind for `waiting_you`, with the failing check's code or the stall's cause in parentheses. `DO` is the first of the reason's `actions` (a `focus` is left out when the run has no pane), written as `workflow status` writes them, with an upper-case placeholder for each value you supply; a run whose only action is `cancel` (a guard a resume cannot get past) shows that. Rows are sorted by longest wait. A gate shows its note in `WHY`, as `gate (note.md)`. **`next_action` is a suggestion for a person who has read the run; a script must not run it unattended** (it can approve a gate or cancel a run). Text is escaped, since run fields can hold agent-written text. Nothing waiting prints `No workflow runs need you.` and exits 0; an unreadable run file is a warning, as in `status --all`, and makes the list incomplete: the exit code is 3, and the text says `No readable workflow run needs you; N could not be read.` in place of the empty line.

`--json` prints `{"schema_version":1,"ok":true,"generated_at":...,"status":"ok","unreadable":0,"runs":[...],"warnings":[]}`; `status` is `partial` (exit 3) when `unreadable` run files were left out. Each row has `run`, `workflow`, `workspace`, `step`, `attempt`, `code` (the reason code, or `stalled` / `idle`), `kind`, `gate_file` (a gate's note, else null), `detail`, `since`, `waiting_seconds` (null when `since` is), `next_action` and `actions` (`{action, args, needs}` as in `workflow status --json`). `workflow status --json` also gains `current.stuck` (`code`, `cause`, `since`, `actions`) for such a run, and no key otherwise.

### Why a line was refused

When the pane refuses a line as `pane_busy` (the first line of a step, a continue line, or a review round's line), the run keeps what the pane looked like, best effort: a run or a daemon from before has none of it. The attempt gets `refusal` (`error`, `message`, `reason` and `seen`), the dispatch reason's `details` carry the same `reason` and `seen`, a refused review round keeps `reason` and `seen` in its `failure`, and `step_dispatched`, `step_continued` and `review_delivery_failed` lines carry `reason` and one `seen_*` key per thing seen (`seen_state`, `seen_state_since`, `seen_changed_by`, `seen_turn_failed`, `seen_turn_failed_kind`, ...). `seen_read_after: true` says the pane was read after the refusal, so it may have moved on. Needs `workflow` 46.

The three commands say it in the same words:

- `workflow attempts` prints a line under a refused attempt: `  refused: the next line was not typed because the pane looked busy: working since 12:01:02, judged from the pane's screen`. The attempt keeps its `refusal` even when a later retry of the line succeeded.
- `workflow status` prints `  refused: the next line was not typed because pane %3 looked busy: working since 12:01:02, judged from the pane's screen` for a refused continue line that waits to be typed again (`current.refusal` in `--json`), and, after the message of a `waiting_you` reason whose step could not be started, `; the pane showed working, judged from the pane's screen`. What set the state reads `judged from the pane's screen` (`screen`), `read back after a daemon restart` (`restore`), `its last turn failed` (`turn_failed`) or `reported by the agent's NAME hook`; a missing `since` is left out. A review round refused as busy adds `    refused: ...` under its `review:` line (`current.review.refusal`).
- `workflow events` prints the flat keys as `key=value`, so the refusal shows as `reason=working seen_state=working seen_changed_by=screen`.

### The status table

`workflow status --table` prints one row per run instead of the full description, and shows finished runs at every scope, not only with `--project` and `--all`. It answers "which of these runs still has a workspace to go back to?".

```
Run                  State               Workspace         Status               Config  tmux session  Worktree  State dir
wr_2610041230559x3k  running (implement) api.worktree-pdf  Live, agent working  yes     yes           yes       yes
wr_2610031100aa11bb  completed           api.worktree-tax  Stopped              yes     no            yes       yes
wr_2610021000cc22dd  cancelled           api.worktree-old  Gone                 no      no            no        yes
```

`kill` leaves the state dir behind, so State dir `yes` on a `Gone` row is normal.

The State column shows the step in parentheses while a run is going. `--json` prints a `rows` document instead (see [Workflow status rows](README.json.md#workflow-status-rows)).

The workspace columns describe the workspace as it is now, not as it was when the run ran. If a workspace was deleted and made again under the same name, an old run reads `Live`. Run files outlive their workspace, so a name with no runs is not an error: it prints `No workflow runs in NAME.` and exits 0.

| Status | Meaning |
|--------|---------|
| `Live, agent working` | The tmux session exists and an agent pane is working. This beats `waiting`: with several agent panes, one blocked on you still reads `working` if another is working |
| `Live, agent waiting` | The session exists and an agent pane waits on you, with none working |
| `Live, agent idle` | The session exists and its agent panes are idle |
| `Live, agent unknown` | The session exists, but the agent daemon did not answer in time or answered with no agent panes, so what the agent is doing is not known |
| `Stopped` | The workspace has a config but no tmux session. `workspace launch` can bring it back when the worktree is still there (Worktree `yes`) |
| `Gone` | No tmux session and no config: the workspace was removed |
| `Unknown` | tmux could not say whether the session exists and the agent daemon did not answer, the session is gone but the config could not be read, or the run's workspace name is unusable |

The other columns say whether each piece exists: `yes`, `no`, or `?` when it could not be read (tmux gave no answer, for example). Worktree is the checkout path stored with the run. A `no` in State dir can also mean nothing was ever written there.

`--project NAME` for a project with no members left is refused with `unknown_workspace`; use `--all` to see its runs. Rows are not cut to the terminal width: use `less -S`, or `--json`.

## Starting from a ticket or a prompt

`workflow start [ID] [REF | --prompt TEXT]` is `start` and `workflow run` in one step, for work that has no workspace yet. `workflow run` needs a workspace; `start` makes one. It takes a `REF`, `--prompt`, or both, not neither; `--name` and `--pane` are refused, because the run goes in the new workspace's own agent pane.

A library recipe can name a workflow, and `workspace start --recipe NAME REF` then makes this same call, with every check below. See [Running a workflow](README.library.md#running-a-workflow).

1. It reads the `REF` as [`start`](README.start.md) does (a JIRA key or URL, a pull request, an issue or a branch) and, for a ticket, reads its title (folded onto one line), body and URL with `gh` or `jira`; for a pull request, also `isCrossRepository`. A pull request from a fork is refused here (below).
2. It picks the branch. With a `REF` it is the ref's own: the JIRA key as written (an existing branch that matches it apart from case is used by its own name; plus the title's words with `--slug`), `issue-<n>` for an issue, `pr-<n>` for a pull request, a plain branch as given. With only `--prompt` it is `--branch`, else the prompt's words with no prefix (`add a hello script` is `add-a-hello-script`). A prompt with no letters or digits is a usage error asking for `--branch`. `--branch` is only for a `--prompt`; with a `REF` it is a usage error.
3. It seeds the definition's inputs. `task` is `KEY: title (url)` for a ticket, the prompt text for a prompt, and the ticket line then the prompt when both are given. `spec` is the path of the ticket body, written to `.workflow/tickets/<key>.md` in the new checkout (listed in `info/exclude`), and only when the ticket has a body. Only inputs the definition declares are seeded, and an `--input` you give always wins.
4. It runs the checks `workflow run` makes (`input_required`, `workflow_command_unset` for a `status: command:` or a `require: command:` that is not `optional: true`, `invalid_input`, and for a `require: exec` entry `workflow_program_missing` and `workflow_test_files_unset`) against the project's main checkout, so a refusal leaves nothing behind. For a workspace it reuses, it also checks that checkout's tree: a workflow with a `guard: unchanged` step refuses a dirty one with `dirty_worktree`, before any agent starts. A checkout it makes starts clean and is not checked.
5. It makes the workspace as `start` does, with no confirmation, no play, no packs and no spine (the run's steps send their own instructions), waits for the coding agent (`--prompt-timeout`), and starts the run in that pane.

A workflow whose `applies_to:` does not list the `REF`'s kind (`jira`, `github_pr`, `github_issue`, `branch`) is refused with `workflow_not_for_ref` (`details.workflow`, `details.kind`, `details.applies_to`) and prints `warning`-style text such as `pr-feedback applies to github_pr, branch, not jira. Pass --force to continue.` The check reads the ref's text only, so it comes before the ticket is read, before the fork check and before anything is made, also under `--dry-run`. `--force` starts anyway and adds the mismatch to `warnings`. In text mode the warning prints on stderr as `warning: ...`; with `--json` it is in `warnings`. `pr-feedback` lists `github_pr` and `branch` (a branch can hold the pull request); a JIRA key or issue is refused. `--prompt` with no `REF` has no kind and is never checked. A workflow with no `applies_to:` applies to everything.

### Routing a ref to a workflow

`workflow start --ref REF` picks the workflow from a table, with no model deciding. `--ref` says the word is a ref, so it is never read as a workflow ID, and adds the single-fit step below; a start with no ID and no `--ref` refuses when no key is set (see [Default workflows](#default-workflows)). It takes no `ID`, no positional `REF` and no `--prompt` (usage errors); `--force` and `--dry-run` work as for any start.

1. The kind comes from `REF` (`jira`, `github_pr`, `github_issue` or `branch`).
2. The config keys win: `workflows.by_ref.<kind>`, then `workflows.default`, read as for [Default workflows](#default-workflows). They are read from the project the ref would join (the parent project when you run it inside a linked worktree) and then the global file; the worktree's own file is not read, since the worktree does not exist yet. Set one with `workspace config set workflows.by_ref.jira rpiv --scope project --name app`.
3. With no key, the definitions that fit the kind are listed: a definition fits when its `applies_to:` lists the kind or is left out. Exactly one fit runs. Several are refused with `no_default_workflow`, listing each with its `use_when`; none fit is refused with the same code. A definition that cannot be read might fit, so while any is broken the single-fit route is never taken: the refusal lists the readable fits and the broken ids (`details.broken`). A valid configured default still routes past a broken unrelated file. Most presets have no `applies_to:`, so a ticket needs a default.

```console
$ workspace config set workflows.by_ref.jira rpiv --scope project --name app
$ workspace workflow start --ref PROJ-101
Routing PROJ-101 (jira) to rpiv, this project's default for jira. Use --dry-run to see the steps.
```

A default that is not a workflow id, or that names no valid definition, is refused with `invalid_workflow_default` (`details.reason` `invalid_value` or `unknown_workflow`), never skipped for another workflow. A default whose `applies_to:` leaves out the kind is refused with reason `not_for_kind`; `--force` starts it anyway with a warning, as for `workflow_not_for_ref`. After routing the start is the same as `workflow start ID REF`: the `applies_to:` check, the ticket read, the fork check and every other check run. Under `--json` the `Routing ...` line goes to stderr; the document's `routed` (`ref`, `kind`, `workflow`, `source` `default` or `only_fit`, `scope` `project` or `global` for a default and `null` for `only_fit`, `key`, `definition_source` `project`, `global` or `builtin`: where the chosen definition was found) says the same. The row has `routed` only when `--ref` was used; without it the key is absent. A config file that does not parse stops the start with its parse error before any routing line is printed. From a repository that is not a registered project the suggested `config set` carries `--scope global`.

A ticket that cannot be read is refused (`ticket_not_found`, `ticket_source_unavailable`) when it was the only source of `task`; with `--prompt` or `--input task=` it is a warning and the run goes on without it. A pull request that cannot be read is always refused, since it can't be checked for a fork. A branch derived from `--prompt` that already exists, locally, on `origin`, or as `.worktrees/<branch>`, is refused with `branch_exists`: pass `--branch NAME` to use it, or another name. `workflow start` needs Claude Code as the workspace's agent (`--agent-kind`, else `agent.kind`); any other kind is refused with `workflow_needs_claude` before anything is made. A workspace that already exists for a `REF`, or for a `--branch` you named, is reused, as `start` does (`would_create` is `false` in the dry run, and `path` is where a branch checked out in another worktree lives), and the run starts in its agent pane.

`workflow start` never checks out a fork's code or starts an agent in it, for any workflow: an agent that starts in a checkout loads its `CLAUDE.md` and `.claude/settings.json` hooks. It refuses with `fork_pull_request`, before any worktree, check or agent, also under `--dry-run`. `details.reason` says which rule refused, `details.next` holds the `workspace start` command to use only if you trust the code, and the refusal is permanent: running it again gives the same answer.

- A pull request `REF` passes only when `gh` says `isCrossRepository` is `false` and the pull request's base (the `host/owner/repo` of its URL) is `origin`'s or `upstream`'s repository, as configured in the project. A remote written as a git `url.<base>.insteadOf` short form is read as git expands it, and an ssh remote's host alias (such as `git@github-work:o/r.git`) as `ssh -G` reports its `hostname`; a name ssh can't resolve, or one that resolves to another host, is compared as written and so refused. `details.reason`: `cross_repository` (from a fork), `unknown_cross_repository` (no boolean in `gh`'s answer, as from an old GitHub Enterprise Server), `other_base_repository` (another repository's pull request, such as `attacker/evil#1`, a bare `#N` that `gh repo set-default` resolves elsewhere, or a URL that isn't that pull request's). `details.pr`, `details.url`, `details.cross_repository`. A pull request that can't be read keeps the read's code.
- A branch it would reuse or adopt, or a local branch with no worktree that `start` would check out, is refused when its git config tracks another repository (read with no network): `branch.<name>.merge` under `refs/pull/` (`pull_ref`), a `branch.<name>.remote` that is a URL (`url_remote`) or names no configured remote (`unknown_remote`), or a remote whose `host/owner/repo` is neither `origin`'s nor `upstream`'s (`other_repository`; a local path or relative remote counts as another). `details.branch`, `details.path` (the project's root for a branch with no worktree).
- A `pr-<n>` branch (or one tracking the local `pr-<n>`) must hold the pull request being started. `start` records the pull request it checked out as `branch.pr-<n>.workspace-pr` (`owner/repo#n`, when the ref named the repository, as `workflow start` always does); another recorded pull request is `other_pull_request`. With no record, `#n` (or the recorded one, for a branch `REF`) is read with `gh` and must pass the pull request rules above, and be the same pull request (`other_pull_request` otherwise), since `start '#n'` drops a fork's upstream and git config can't tell. A read that fails keeps its code (`ticket_not_found`, `ticket_source_unavailable`) with `details.branch` and `details.path`.

Tracking config is a proxy for where a branch's code came from, not proof: a branch fetched by hand with no upstream (`git fetch alice feature:feature`) can't be told apart from your own. GitHub Enterprise Server pull request URLs are not refs `start` reads (they are refused as invalid branch names); use `#N` from a checkout of that repository.

If you trust the code, `workspace start '#N'` (or `workspace start BRANCH`) makes or adopts the workspace, and `workspace workflow run ID` in it starts the workflow. Its agent then starts in that code and loads its `CLAUDE.md` and `.claude/settings.json` hooks. For a bare `#N`, the checkout names the repository `gh pr view` answered from (`owner/repo#N`), so it can't check out another remote's pull request than the one checked; on another host than github.com it stays `#N`, which `gh` resolves from the same repository config.

Once the workspace exists, a failure never removes it. The error keeps its code and gains `details.workspace`, `details.path` and `details.next`, an array holding one command: the `workflow run` command with the same inputs and note, which starts the workflow in that workspace by hand. This covers `agent_not_ready` (the agent was not ready in time) and a refusal from the run itself such as `pane_has_run`, and `start_failed` (the session did not start). A failure inside `start` itself, once the checkout exists, carries `details.path` and a `next` that is `cd PATH && workspace workflow run ...`, as the workspace name is not known.

### Default workflows

`workspace go` chooses a workflow the same way and then starts it; see [go](README.go.md).

`workflow start` can leave out the `ID`. Config then names the workflow, by the kind of `REF`:

1. `workflows.by_ref.<kind>`, where `<kind>` is `jira`, `github_pr`, `github_issue` or `branch`, or `prompt` for a start with `--prompt` and no `REF`.
2. `workflows.default`, for any kind that has no `by_ref` key.

All six keys can be set globally or per project (`workspace config set workflows.by_ref.jira rpiv --scope project --name app`). A project's keys count only in a checkout your config ties to that project: a clone that merely shares its directory name reads the global keys. A global `by_ref` key beats a project `workflows.default`, because the more specific key is read first. `workflow start` reads the global and project layers only. It does not read a worktree's own setting, because the worktree is not chosen until the ticket has been read, and the ticket read needs the workflow.

With one argument, what it means depends on the word:

| You type | It is read as |
| --- | --- |
| `workflow start rpiv` | the workflow `rpiv`, when a workflow of that id exists. It still needs a `REF` or `--prompt`. |
| `workflow start PROJ-101` | a JIRA ref, so config chooses the workflow |
| `workflow start '#123'` or a URL | a pull request or issue ref, so config chooses the workflow |
| `workflow start fix-uploads` | a branch, so config chooses the workflow, and a warning says so |
| `workflow start fix-uploads --prompt "..."` | a mistyped workflow id: `unknown_workflow` |
| `workflow start --prompt "..."` | a prompt, so config chooses the workflow |

With no workflow named and no key set for the kind, the start is refused with `no_default_workflow` before anything is made. `details.kind` is the kind, `details.ref` is the word that was read as the ref (null for `--prompt`), and `details.known` lists the workflows that exist. A key that holds an id no workflow has is `unknown_workflow`, and `workspace config validate` warns about it ahead of time as `unknown_default_workflow`.

Three things to know:

- With a default set, a mistyped workflow id becomes a branch. `workflow start rpvi` starts the default workflow on a new branch named `rpvi`. It prints `Warning: ...` naming the workflow config chose and the known ids, and the same text is in `results[0].warnings` under `--json`. A word shaped like a ticket does not warn. Run it with `--dry-run` first to see the branch and the workflow.
- A branch named like a workflow needs the two-argument form. If a branch is called `explore`, `workflow start explore` runs the workflow `explore`. Use `workflow start rpiv explore` to run `rpiv` on the branch.
- A global `by_ref` key beats a project `workflows.default`. To change one project's workflow for every kind, set the project's `workflows.by_ref.<kind>` keys too, or remove the global `by_ref` key.

```sh
workspace config set workflows.default rpiv
workspace config set workflows.by_ref.github_pr pr-feedback
workspace workflow start PROJ-101 --dry-run --json     # rpiv, from workflows.default
workspace workflow start '#123' --dry-run              # pr-feedback, from workflows.by_ref.github_pr
```

## Definitions

A definition is a YAML file named for its id. These places are searched, in this order, and the first file with the id is the one every command reads:

1. The project's own, `~/.config/workspace/projects/NAME/workflows/<id>.yml`, for the project the command works for.
2. The user's, `~/.config/workspace/workflows/<id>.yml`.
3. The directories listed in the `workflows.sources` config key, in list order (see [Installing from sources](#installing-from-sources)).
4. The presets that ship with workspace.

So copying a preset into either directory and editing it replaces it, for that project or for every project. Each row of `workflow show` and each `show ID` says which place the definition came from (`project`, `global` or `builtin`).

#### Project definitions

NAME is the project: a worktree workspace (`proj.worktree-x`) reads its parent project's directory, the same rule that gives it the project's settings. Every command gets the project from one place: `--name WORKSPACE` (a name with no config is refused as `unknown_workspace`, not read as "no project"), else the project of the current directory (`show`, `validate`, `render`, `run`, `add`, `update`, `remove`), or of the checkout a `start` joins (and so `--ref` routing), or the `--name` or `--project` scope (`stats`). Two questions are kept apart. Which workspace a directory is (the target of `run`, `render` and `check`): a `.workspace-project` file is believed when that workspace's own config is rooted at this checkout, so a worktree `start` made, even in a clone, targets itself. May the checkout read a project's definitions: a directory counts only when your config ties the project to its repository: the project's own main config (`NAME.yml`, never a `NAME.worktree-X` config, which `start` writes for whatever checkout it runs in) has a root with the same git common dir as the directory. That holds whether the name comes from the state, a `.workspace-project` file or the checkout's directory name, so a stopped checkout with no state entry works, in `show` as in `start`, while a clone at `evil/myapp`, or a worktree `start` made in it, is not `myapp`. With no main config there is no project. Failing that tie means no project definitions, never a refusal; only a marker whose workspace is rooted elsewhere is refused. A registered project that is not a git checkout ties by its root, for the root directory itself. The warning of a change to a definition leaves out a project directory that cannot be read. A project with no config at all (an unregistered checkout) has no project definitions and routes with the global `workflows.by_ref.<kind>` and `workflows.default` only; a project config for one exists only by hand-editing. With no project (a directory outside any checkout) the lookup is the user's directory and the presets. A `.workspace-project` file is a claim made by the repository, so it picks a project only when your own config ties that project to this checkout (the project's stored root is in the same repository); any other is ignored, with a warning on stderr, and nothing of that project is read. `run`, `render` and `check` with no `--name` also refuse a marker at the checkout's own root (`not_in_workspace`) instead of acting on the workspace it names; pass `--name`. A marker above the checkout belongs to an outer checkout: there is no project here, and the user's and the preset definitions still work. A NAME that could not be one directory name (a `/`, a leading `.` or `-`, a space, odd characters) has no directory, and the command warns on stderr. Nothing makes the directory: create it by hand (`mkdir -p ~/.config/workspace/projects/NAME/workflows`).

`workflow add`, `update` and `remove` write only the user's directory, never a project's. Inside a project they say so when a project file is involved: `add` says a project definition of the id hides what it saved in that project; `update` says the same as `add` when a project definition hides the file it wrote; `update` and `remove` of an id that only the project has refuse with `unknown_workflow` and name the project file (change it by hand); `update` of an id both have, given the sha256 of the project file that `show ID --source` printed, fails `stale_workflow` and names both files and both shas (`details.project_path`, `details.project_sha256`; the user's file is `details.path`, `details.sha256`), and passes with the user file's sha; `remove` says the project definition stays in effect in that project (`preset` is then false, `project_path` the file) instead of claiming the preset is back.

Definitions are never read from a repository, whatever the checkout holds. A step's `status:` is a shell command that runs on your machine; a definition checked into a repository would let a branch or a fork choose what runs there. To share one with a team, copy the file into each person's config directory.

A project's definition can `extends:` a user's or a preset's, and be extended by them in that project; the parents are searched project first, then the user's, then the presets, and a project file that extends the id it hides reaches the user's or the preset's one. `check` and `simulate` read the project's definitions too.

A project's file hides the same id everywhere else for that project, in every command: `show`, `validate`, `run`, `render`, `start` and `--ref` routing (where it also counts as a fit or, when broken, as a definition that might fit; see [routing](#routing-a-ref-to-a-workflow)). A run keeps a copy of the definition it started with, so `status`, `events`, `attempts`, `audit` and `compare` read that copy. `stats ID` counts the runs of one id together: two projects with their own `team.yml` are mixed unless `--name` or `--project` narrows it; with a scope, `--json` `definition` and a text line (for a project file) say where that project's definition of the id is found now.

```yaml
schema_version: 0.1
id: ship
title: Plan, build, verify
description: A small change with a plan gate.
inputs:
  task: {description: What to build, required: true}
include: [review]            # packs added after the default ones
instructions: |              # for every step
  You are working on {{inputs.task}} in {{workspace}}, branch {{branch}}.
  Put this run's files under {{artifacts}}/.
max_attempts: 6
steps:
  plan:
    prompt: Write {{artifacts}}/plan.md. Don't change code.
    produces: [plan.md]
    gate: approve
    timeout: 45m
  build:
    prompt: Implement {{artifacts}}/plan.md.
    uses: [devenv]
  verify:
    prompt: Run the tests and review the diff. Write {{artifacts}}/verify.md.
    produces: [verify.md]
    uses: [devenv, test-db]
    status: {command: test}
    on_fail: {goto: build, max: 3, context: continue}
```

Top-level keys:

| Key | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `schema_version` | `0.1`, the current version, or left out, which reads as `0.1`; `1` is still accepted as the earlier spelling of `0.1`. The version is compared as written, so `0.10` is not `0.1`. Later versions will be added beside it, and a value that names none of them is refused with the list of versions supported |
| `id` | The file's name without `.yml`, or left out |
| `title`, `description` | For `workflow show` |
| `inputs` | Input name to `description`, `required` (default false) and `pattern` (a Ruby regexp source, at most 300 characters, matched against the whole value as `\A(?:PATTERN)\z`; it must compile, else `invalid_workflow`). `run --input NAME=VALUE` supplies them; one that is not required and not given is empty. A set value that doesn't match, or (for an input with a pattern) is over 4096 characters, is refused with `invalid_input` (details `workflow`, `input`, `pattern`, never the value) before any run exists, also under `run --dry-run` and `start`, seeded values included; only a truly empty value skips the check (whitespace is checked). `.` does not match a newline, so prefer explicit character classes such as `[0-9]+` or `[\w.-]+`; the match is cut off after 1 second on Ruby 3.2 and later |
| `include` | Pack names composed after the default packs, for every step |
| `instructions` | Text for every step |
| `max_attempts` | Attempts the run may make across all its steps before a failure stops it (default 6) |
| `budget` | `{tokens: N, action: stop}`, `{tokens: N, action: interrupt}`, or left out (the default, no budget): the tokens the run may use before the next attempt is held. `tokens` is a whole number from 1 up; `action` is `stop` (the default) or `interrupt`, which also has the sweep press the agent's interrupt keys on an attempt that spends the allowance (needs `workflow` 49). See [Token budgets](#token-budgets) |
| `timeout` | A duration such as `8h`, at most 365 days (`8760h`), or left out (the default, no run timeout): the active time the run may use before the next attempt is held. The clock starts at the first attempt (or an earlier `waiting_lock`) and counts only active time: running, checking and reviewing with no stored reason, `waiting_lock` and `retry_backoff`. Every other stored stop (a gate, a `parked` resume, a pause, `budget_spent`, a failed check) is a person's and does not count; nor does time the machine was off or asleep, nor the time a step is stalled (see [Active time](#active-time)). A stop that is never stored, such as a permission prompt the agent sits at, **does** count. A run past it is held at the next attempt (see [Run timeouts](#run-timeouts)); an attempt under way is never stopped by it here. Needs `workflow` 33. |
| `timeout_action` | `hold` (the default) or `interrupt`: what happens past the timeout. `hold` stops the next attempt from starting; `interrupt` also lets `workflow sweep` press the interrupt keys of the agent working past the limit, once, under the sweep cause `run_timed_out`. Warns `timeout_action_without_timeout` without a `timeout`. Needs `workflow` 33. |
| `stall` | A duration such as `20m`, or left out: how long a step's pane may sit idle, its agent's turn over, before `workflow sweep` decides the step. Only the sweep reads it. Left out, the sweep uses the global `workflows.default_stall` (10 minutes unless you set it; `off` or `0` turns the default off, so a definition with no `stall` is never decided for being idle). `workflow show ID` prints the stall a workflow runs with |
| `redeliver` | A whole number from 0 to 10 (default 3): how many times `workflow sweep` types a step's line again when it was not delivered (see [Sweeping stopped runs](#sweeping-stopped-runs)). `0` turns the retry off. A run stored before the key existed reads as `0` and is never retried |
| `version` | A whole number of 1 or more, the revision of this definition; `workflow show` prints it and a run stores it in its definition. Left out: none. Not inherited through `extends`; a child sets its own. Needs `workflow` 55 |
| `use_when` | One line (at most 200 characters) saying when to pick the workflow; shown by `workflow show` and listed by `workflow show --json`. Left out: none |
| `applies_to` | A list of the ref kinds the workflow is for: `jira`, `github_pr`, `github_issue`, `branch`. `workflow start` with a `REF` of another kind is refused with `workflow_not_for_ref` unless `--force`. Left out (the default): every kind, no check. A run stored before the key existed has none |
| `deny` | A list of shell-glob patterns (at most 50, each at most 200 characters with at most 8 `*` or `?`) for Bash commands the run's agent may not run; see [Denying commands](#denying-commands). A run stored before the key existed has none |
| `reviewers` | Reviewer name (lowercase letters, digits and `_`, starting with a letter) to `{title, prompt or prompt_file, include, parallel}`: the named reviewers a step's `review:` picks from. `prompt` or `prompt_file` (exactly one) is what the reviewer is asked; `include` is packs, as for a step; `parallel` (default true) says whether it runs in the parallel batch or alone after it. A prompt may also use `{{review.subject}}`, `{{review.revision}}`, `{{review.round}}`, `{{review.verdict_path}}` and `{{review.previous}}`. Left out: none. A run stored before the key existed has none. Parsed and stored only; nothing in a run acts on it yet. Needs `workflow` 33. |
| `extends` | The id of another workflow to start from (a user's file, then a preset; see [Extending a workflow](#extending-a-workflow)). Left out: none |
| `replace` | With `extends`: step id to the whole new step, swapped in at the parent's place. See [Extending a workflow](#extending-a-workflow) |
| `steps` | Step id to step, in the order they run. With `extends`, the steps to add |

Step keys:

| Key | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `title` | For `workflow show` and `status` |
| `prompt` or `prompt_file` | What the step asks for. `prompt_file` is a path beside the definition, read when the run starts |
| `include` | Pack names composed for this step |
| `produces` | Files the step must write, as paths under the run's artifacts directory |
| `status` | A check run in the checkout when the turn ends: a command line, `{run: COMMAND}`, or `{command: test}` / `{command: lint}` for the project's own [`commands.test` or `commands.lint`](README.config.md); an unset one is refused at the start (`workflow_command_unset`), as is a non-optional `require: command:`. `timeout` (default `20m`) stops it; a `timeout` with no value is refused. `report_code: true` lets the check name its own failure code: the log's last `code: NAME` line (a code the engine hands out itself is never taken) becomes the failure's `code` with `code_source: check`, instead of the plain `check_failed` |
| `gate` | `approve`: wait for `workflow approve` after the step passes |
| `guard` | `unchanged` or `config`: the engine checks the checkout when the agent's turn ends (see [The guard](#the-guard)) |
| `require` | A list of proofs checked after `produces` and before `status` (see [Required proofs](#required-proofs)); `exec: PROGRAM ARGS` with an optional `timeout:` runs a program of your own, with no shell |
| `uses` | Lock names the run holds for the step. `dev-env` is `devenv`; `edit` is refused (only the agent that edits can hold it). A step that uses `devenv` is not sent the `dev` pack, even when it is in the defaults or an `include:`: the run holds the environment for it |
| `on_fail` | `{goto: STEP, max: N}`: on failure go back to an earlier step, at most `N` times (default 1). `context:` overrides the target step's `context` for that jump. `log_tail: N` (1 to 200) adds the last `N` lines of the failed check's log to the retry's instructions, and `backoff: DURATION` (`90`, `30s`, `2m`, `1h`; more than 0, at most 24 hours) delays the retry; see [Retrying a failed step](#retrying-a-failed-step). `except: [NAME, ...]` (at most 8 names, each at most 60 characters) names failure codes or causes that never loop: a failure whose `code` or `cause` is listed stops the run instead of going back |
| `context` | `fresh` (default): start the step in a new conversation. `continue`: keep the pane's conversation |
| `timeout` | How long the step may take, in active time (see [Active time](#active-time)), before `status` flags it `timed_out`. Nothing is stopped unless `on_timeout` says so and `workflow sweep` runs |
| `after` | Only in a step added by a workflow with `extends`: the id of the step it goes after (default: the end). A step that is not added that way with `after` is a problem |
| `max_attempts` | A whole number of 1 or more, or left out (the default, no cap of its own): the most attempts this step may make in the run. The run's `max_attempts` still applies. `validate` warns when it can't hold (see [Validating a definition](#validating-a-definition)). Needs `workflow` 33. |
| `review` | Who reviews the step's work before it passes: a list of reviewer names (shorthand for `from:` with every default), or a mapping of `from` (reviewer names from the workflow's `reviewers`, none twice), `need` (approvals required, 1 to the size of `from`, default all of them), `must` (reviewers who must be among the approvals, a subset of `from`, `need` at least its size, default none), `subject` (text saying what is reviewed; may use `{{review.revision}}`, `{{review.round}}`, `{{review.verdict_path}}` and `{{review.previous}}`), `revision` (a command whose output is added to the revision, optional), `max_rounds` (1 to 50, default 6), `timeout` (a duration up to 24h, default 30m), `uses` (lock names, default none), `context` (`fresh` or `continue`, default `fresh`) and `notify` (default true). An unknown reviewer, a bad or repeated `need`, `must` or `from` entry, an empty `review: {}`, and a null written for a key that has a default are `invalid_workflow` (null `subject` or `revision` means none). A step with a `review` counts as having a proof, so `validate` does not warn `step_without_proof`. Left out: none. In a run, once the step's own checks (guard, `produces`, `require:`, `status:`) pass, its attempt ends with outcome `checks_passed` and the step reads `reviewing` while a review round runs. An approving round rewrites the attempt's outcome to `passed` (with `reviews` and `review_outcome`, `{round, approvals, need, must, passed}`; `approvals`, `need` and `must` are `null` until verdicts are counted; `revision`, the revision the round judged, needs `workflow` 38) and the step passes to its gate or the next step; a rejecting one makes it `failed` (code `review_rejected`) and runs the step again as a new attempt told what the reviewers wrote (see [The rejection loop](#the-rejection-loop)), until the step has spent `max_rounds`, when the run stops with the reason `review_rejected`; a round that can't be decided stops it with `review_incomplete`, the step still `reviewing`. A round is not an attempt and does not count against `max_attempts`; each retry of the author is one. Each round runs in a review window of its own (see [A review round's window](#a-review-rounds-window)). Needs `workflow` 33. |
| `on_timeout` | `warn` (the default), `interrupt` or `fail`: what a step past its `timeout` gets when `workflow sweep` runs: nothing (`warn`), the agent's interrupt keys once (`interrupt`), or a failed step (`fail`). Without a sweep every value behaves as `warn`. `interrupt` or `fail` with no `timeout` is a `workflow validate` warning |

Text in `instructions` and `prompt` may use `{{workspace}}`, `{{branch}}`, `{{artifacts}}` (the run's directory, `<checkout>/.workflow/<run>`), `{{run}}` and `{{inputs.NAME}}`. Any other placeholder is a problem in the definition. A definition with problems is reported with all of them at once (code `invalid_workflow`).

### Extending a workflow

`extends: ID` starts a definition from another one, so a variant states only what differs. `rpiv-auto-approve` is `rpiv` with a replaced `plan` step and an added `plan_review` step:

```yaml
extends: rpiv
max_attempts: 32
replace:
  plan:                      # the whole new step, in plan's place
    prompt: ...
    produces: [plan.md]
steps:
  plan_review:               # a new step
    after: plan              # goes after plan; without after: it goes last
    prompt: ...
    produces: [plan-approval.md]
```

How the result is built: the parent's top-level keys, then this file's over them (a key it sets replaces the parent's value whole), except `inputs` and `reviewers`, which merge by name with this file's winning (a reviewer this file names replaces the parent's whole entry, and a step of this file may name a reviewer of the parent; the names are checked after the merge). `replace:` swaps a parent step whole (nothing of the old step is kept, so say `gate:` again if you want it); each added step goes after the step its `after:` names, or at the end, and several added after the same step keep the order written. `id` and `version` are always this file's, never the parent's. A parent's `prompt_file:`, on a step or a reviewer, is read from the parent's directory. A `replace:` step that leaves out the `review:` of the step it replaces gets the `review_dropped` warning.

The parent is found like any workflow id (`~/.config/workspace/workflows/` first, then the presets), so your own file named `rpiv` replaces the preset for every workflow that extends it, `rpiv-auto-approve` included. A file may extend the id it shadows: your `rpiv.yml` with `extends: rpiv` starts from the preset `rpiv` (only the sources after its own are searched), which is the way to change a preset and keep the rest. `show` prints each parent with its source and path, so you can see which file was read. A parent may extend another, up to 4 above the one you read.

Refused with `invalid_workflow`. A parent that can't be used (missing, a cycle, a chain over 4, an `extends` that is not an id, or itself invalid) is reported alone, each of its problems under `extends ID (PATH)`, with the parent's own id and file, because nothing else in the child can be judged until it works. With a good parent, all of the child's problems are listed together: `replace` naming a step the parent lacks (the message lists its steps), an added step with an id the parent already has (use `replace`), an `after` naming no step, `replace` or `after` without `extends`. The merged result is then checked as any definition is, so a `goto` to a step that no longer comes earlier is a problem too.

A run stores the resolved definition, so editing the parent later changes nothing in a run that is going. `definition_sha256` is the digest of the file's text alone when it extends nothing; with `extends` it is the digest of the file's own digest and each parent's text, so `workflow compare --by definition` tells apart runs of the same child file over a changed parent. For the same reason the runs of `rpiv-auto-approve` stored before it began to extend `rpiv` form their own group in `workflow compare`: their digest covered the old file. `workflow show` prints one `Extends:` line per parent (source and path); `show --json` and `validate --json` add `extends` (the parents nearest first: `id`, `source`, `path`, `sha256`) and `resolved_sha256`, the digest a run started now would store, and keep `sha256` as the file's own, the one `update --if-sha256` wants.

`workflow add`, `update` and `remove` of a definition that others extend print a warning on stderr naming them, and their action row gains `extended_by` (the ids, then `ID (project NAME)` for each project definition that extends it: every project's directory is scanned, whatever the current directory, unless that project has its own definition of the id); the children are not changed, and read the new file the next time they are loaded.

### Validating a definition

`workflow validate PATH` reads the file; `workflow validate -` reads stdin. It runs the same checks `show` and `run` do, reports every problem at once, and writes nothing (it does not touch `~/.config/workspace/workflows/`). A PATH that sits in the project's workflows directory, the user's or the presets' is reported with its `scope` (`project`, `global` or `builtin`; the text adds "in the project definitions"), and gets a `shadowed` warning when a file of that id in an earlier place is the one a run reads. It exits 0 when the text is valid and 1 with `invalid_workflow` when it is not. A file that is not UTF-8 text or not a YAML mapping fails the same way; a PATH that can't be read is a usage error.

A valid text can also get **warnings**, which never change the exit code and never stop `start` or `run`. Today there are fourteen. `timeout_action_without_timeout`: the workflow sets `timeout_action` but has no `timeout`, so it never applies (`step` is `null`). `on_timeout_without_timeout`: a step sets `on_timeout: interrupt` or `fail` but has no `timeout`, so it never applies. `step_without_proof`: a step with no `produces`, no `status`, no `require` and no `gate` passes on any turn end, even one that did nothing, so validate names the step (`warning: step build has no produces, status, require or gate, ...`) and says what to add: a file it must write, proofs about the repository, a command that must pass, or `gate: approve` so a person decides. A gate counts as proof because the run waits for the person after the turn ends. No shipped preset warns on its own text; `rpiv` and `rpiv-auto-approve` use `commands.test`, so they get `command_unset` in a project without it. Three more warnings concern reviews. `review_dropped`: a `replace:` step swaps a parent step that had a `review:` for one with none, so nothing reviews it now; repeat `review:` in the replacing step to keep it. `review_rounds_reach_step_attempts`: a reviewed step's `review.max_rounds` is at or above its own `max_attempts`, so one attempt lost to its `status:` check or an undelivered line cuts the last round. `review_rounds_exceed_run_attempts`: `review.max_rounds` is above what the workflow's `max_attempts` leaves after one attempt for each other step. A rejection starts a new author attempt, so rounds spend attempts. The defaults trigger it: `max_rounds` is 6 and `max_attempts` is 6, so any reviewed step in a workflow of two or more steps warns until you set `max_attempts` (the message gives the number) or lower `review.max_rounds`. `on_fail_exceeds_max_attempts` counts a reviewed step before the failing one, or inside the loop, as `max_rounds` attempts instead of one (the message then says `if every review round is used`, since the count is a worst case and no longer a floor). Three more warnings weigh limits against each other. `on_fail_exceeds_max_attempts`: a step's `on_fail.max` retries can never all start under `max_attempts`.
