The run spends one attempt per step it delivers, so step N (counting from 1) first fails on attempt N at the earliest, and each retry delivers the steps from `goto` to it again (`span` steps). Retry M can start only while fewer than `max_attempts` attempts are made, so the rule warns when `N + (M - 1) * span >= max_attempts`; the message says how many retries fit and the `max_attempts` that fits them all. `--extra-attempts` on a run can still raise the limit. `on_fail_exceeds_step_attempts`: the step an `on_fail` goes back to sets its own `max_attempts` below `on_fail.max + 1`, the times it runs once the loop is used. `step_attempts_exceed_run`: a step's `max_attempts` is above the workflow's, so the run stops first. Each `on_fail` is checked alone: three steps with `max_attempts: 8` and `on_fail` `max: 3` and `max: 2` stay quiet even when both loops together can't fit. `unknown_pack`: an `include:` (the workflow's, a reviewer's or a step's) or a default pack (`workflows.defaults.include`, added to every step; `step` is `null`) names a pack the library can't find. For the workflow's, the defaults and the first step, `start` would refuse it; `start` reads only the first step's packs, so for a later step it passes and the run stops when it reaches that step, which the message says. A pack name that can't be a name at all (`Bad/Name`, `{{task}}`) is a warning everywhere. `command_unset`: a step's `status: command:` or a `require: command:` not marked `optional` names `test` or `lint`, and the project has no `commands.test`/`commands.lint`, which `start` there would refuse as `workflow_command_unset`. The hint names `--name` when the project is a workspace, and otherwise says to run `workspace add .` first and then `workspace config set`. A pack named by the workflow, the defaults or the first step is warned about once, not again for a later step. Three more concern a `require: exec` entry. `exec_program_missing`: its program is not on the PATH of the process that reads it (or an absolute or `~/` path is not an executable file), which `start` would refuse as `workflow_program_missing`; the daemon's PATH can differ from the one `validate` sees. `test_files_unset`: it uses `{{changed.tests}}` and the project has no `commands.test_files`, which `start` would refuse as `workflow_test_files_unset`. `exec_list_without_dashes`: it has a list placeholder with no `--` argument before it, so a changed file named like an option would be read as one. These two read the project of the current directory, so they are warnings (the definition may be started in another project, and a project library can hold the pack). Outside a git checkout there is no project: the checks that need one are not raised, and the document lists what was left unchecked in `not_checked` (`pack_not_checked`, for a pack that is not built in or global; `commands_not_checked`, which also appears, with the parse error, when the project's own config file doesn't parse; `exec_program_not_checked`, for a relative program such as `script/check`, which needs the checkout to resolve against; `test_files_not_checked`, for `{{changed.tests}}` outside a checkout or when `commands.test_files` can't be parsed. A bare or absolute program is still judged against this process's PATH and HOME; a broken global config is read as defaults and reported nowhere), printed as `not checked: ...` lines. A definition that needs neither check has an empty `not_checked`. Scripts read warnings from `workflow validate --json` (the `warnings` and `not_checked` arrays); no other workflow command reports them.

The id the text is checked as is `--id`, else a string `id:` in the text, else the file's name without its extension, else `stdin` for `-`, or `file` for a file whose name is not an id. An `id:` that differs from `--id` is a problem. A relative `prompt_file:` is read from the file's directory for a PATH and from the current directory for `-`. The problems name the paths they looked at, never their contents.

### Rendering a step's instructions

`workflow render ID STEP` prints what the agent of step STEP would be typed to read: the packs, the workflow's `instructions:` and the step's prompt with its `{{placeholders}}` filled, and the line about the files it must produce, as `start` composes them for the step's first attempt. It runs the engine's own composing code, so it can't drift from a delivery; a spec compares the two. It writes nothing (no run file, event, binding, lock, worktree or `.workflow/` directory) and calls neither tmux nor the agent daemon, so it works outside a tmux session. `STEP` is any step of the definition, not only the first.

Inputs are checked as `start` checks them (`--input KEY=VALUE`, repeatable): an input the workflow doesn't have is a usage error, a value that misses its `pattern:` is `invalid_input`, and a required input left out is refused with `input_required` rather than rendered with `{{inputs.task}}` unfilled, since text with holes in it looks like a prompt that works. An unknown step is `unknown_step` (`details.workflow`, `details.step`, `details.steps`); an unknown workflow, an invalid definition and an unknown pack give `unknown_workflow`, `invalid_workflow` and `unknown_library_entry` as they do elsewhere.

The checkout the packs and the project's `commands.test`/`commands.lint` are read from is the current workspace's (`--name` picks another). Outside a git checkout and any workspace the render still works from the current directory, with `{{workspace}}` shown as `<workspace>` and a `not_in_checkout` warning, since the project's own packs and commands were not read.

Some values belong to a run that does not exist. These are previews: `{{run}}` is `wr_preview`, `{{artifacts}}` (and the binding's artifacts and instructions paths) lie under `.workflow/wr_preview/`, the attempt is 1, and `{{workspace}}` is `<workspace>` when none was found. The text names them on stderr; `--json` lists them under `previews`. Text the runner adds under `## For this attempt` is not added, because it comes from what happens during a run: the run's `--note`; a retry's text (a failed check's log, a rejected gate's note, a backoff's text); a `resume --note`; and, for the step after a gate, the approval's `--note` and the line "The approved note is PATH, sha256 ..." that names the gate's note (`pr-feedback` `fix`, `investigate` `fix`, `explore` `report`). A real first attempt can therefore carry that section: `workflow run --note` reaches attempt 1 of the first step, and an approval or a resume reaches the step it starts. A render never does. A step with `gate: approve` is rendered like any other; stderr says the run would wait after its turn.

On a terminal the text is written with control, bidi and zero-width characters escaped (`\e`, `\u202E`), line by line, tabs kept, because a pack, a definition or an input can hold them. Piped or redirected it is written as is, so it can be saved or diffed. Warnings and preview values are escaped the same way.

### Drawing a workflow

`workflow graph ID` draws a definition: a node for each step, a gate node after a step with `gate: approve`, a review node after a step with `review:`, and an edge for each way a run moves between them. The edges are `next`, `fail` (labelled with its `max`, and `backoff` and `except` when set), `reject` (a gate sends its step back; the label says `--to` can name an earlier step, since that is chosen when the person rejects) and `review_rejected` (a review sends its step back, labelled with `max_rounds`). It reads the definition only, writes nothing and needs no run or tmux session. `--name` picks the workspace whose project's definitions are read first. A step that came from an `extends:` parent carries that parent's id as its `origin` annotation in the model. A `--run` graph draws the run's stored definition, which records no parents, so its steps carry no `origin`.

```
      (Start)
        |
        v
      +----------+
      | Research |
      +----------+
        |
        v
      +------+
   +->| Plan |
   |  +------+
   |    |
   |    v
   |  +===========+
   +--| Plan gate |  reject (or --to an earlier step)
      +===========+
        |
        v
      +-----------+
+---->| Implement |
|     +-----------+
|       |
|       v
|     +--------+
+-----| Verify |  fail: max 3
      +--------+
        |
        v
      (End)

Run limits: max_attempts 10
```

`--format` picks the drawing: `ascii` (the default, readable anywhere), `mermaid` (a `stateDiagram-v2` to paste into a Markdown file), `dot` (Graphviz input: `workspace workflow graph rpiv --format dot | dot -Tsvg`) or `json` (the model the others draw). The run limits that apply to every step (`max_attempts`, `timeout`, `timeout_action`, `stall`, `budget`) are the legend: a `Run limits:` line in ASCII, a note in Mermaid and the graph label in DOT.

`--run RUN` draws the definition the run stored, not the file in the catalog, which may have changed since, and marks where the run stands. Each step, gate and review node is `passed`, `current`, `waiting`, `failed` or `pending`; each step shows its passes and attempts, and each `fail` edge the loops the run took. A failed step that is the run's current step while the run waits reads `waiting`: the run waits there, and the attempts show the failures. A gate or review rejected with `--to` another step is `pending` while the run goes on and `failed` once it ended. The run must be of workflow ID: another workflow's run is refused with `usage`, an unknown RUN with `unknown_run`. A run's stored definition carries no `extends:` origin, so a `--run` drawing has none.

### Trying a step's check

`workflow check ID STEP [--artifacts DIR]` runs the `status:` command of step STEP once, outside any run, so a check can be tested without spending a run: `workspace workflow check rpiv-auto-approve plan_review --artifacts ./fixture` runs the check of `plan_review` against the files in `./fixture`. It goes through the engine's own lookup of the command and `WorkflowCheck`, the runner a run's check uses: `sh -c` in the checkout's root (even from a subdirectory), the step's `timeout` (default 20 minutes), and the same three environment variables, with `WORKSPACE_RUN_ID` the preview id `wr_preview`, `WORKSPACE_RUN_ARTIFACTS` the artifacts directory and `WORKSPACE_RUN_BASE_SHA` the checkout's HEAD (empty when it can't be read). So a check of the form `git log BASE..HEAD` sees no commits here, where a run's check would see the run's own. A checkout means the working tree: inside its `.git` directory the check runs but warns `not_in_checkout`. A `status:` command is never rendered, only given those environment variables, so a check takes no inputs and `--input` is refused. The `status: {command: test|lint}` form runs the project's `commands.test`/`commands.lint`; an unset one is refused with `workflow_command_unset`, whose message prints the `config set` line (`--name` the project's workspace, the one the value is read from, even when `check` was given a worktree's `--name`; the value is the placeholder `"<test command>"`, which fails if pasted as it is. Unless that project is a workspace that exists, it says only to set the key in the project's workspace and prints no command). A step with no `status:` is refused with `step_without_status`, an unknown step with `unknown_step`. It does not run `require:` proofs.

**Trust.** The command is a shell command from a definition, run in the current checkout (`--name` picks another) with your permissions, the same trust as a run. Definitions come only from the catalog (`~/.config/workspace/workflows/`, then the presets), never from a path given here.

**Artifacts.** `--artifacts DIR` must be an existing directory (a relative path is taken from the current directory); otherwise it is `artifacts_not_directory`. It is what `WORKSPACE_RUN_ARTIFACTS` holds, and workspace never writes to it. Without `--artifacts` an empty directory is made in a new temporary directory and reported on stderr (`artifacts_created` in `--json`), so a check that reads a file fails the way it would in a run that wrote none. The check log is always written in that temporary directory (`workspace-check-*` under the system temp directory), never in a run's area, and is left in place; its path is reported. The next check removes this user's `workspace-check-*` directories a day old or more (links are not followed). The log's size is not bounded, as a run's check log is not. A check that runs for more than a day can lose its directory to another check's sweep. An empty `--artifacts ''` is refused like any other path that is not a directory. Nothing else changes: no run file, event, binding or lock. A step's `uses:` locks are not taken, so a check that needs the dev environment or test database must be run with the same care as running that command by hand.

**Output and exit.** The last 40 lines of the check's output go to the output (escaped through `TerminalText` on a terminal, as `render` does); a summary line (passed or failed, the exit code or timeout, the duration, the log and artifacts paths) goes to stderr. Exit 0 when the check passes, 1 when it fails, times out or can't start. `--json` prints `ok: true` with a `passed` boolean (the check ran; as `workflow audit` prints `clean`) and exits 0 or 1 by it.

### Simulating a workflow

`workflow simulate ID --script FILE` walks the real engine through a workflow with a scripted fake agent and prints every transition, so a loop, a retry's backoff, a gate or a token budget can be tried without spending a run:

```
workspace workflow simulate rpiv --script fail-verify-twice.yml --input task=x
```

The engine is the real one (`WorkflowEngine`); what it touches is replaced. The run file, its history, the checkout and the artifacts live in a temporary directory that is removed afterwards. The pane is only a name. There is no tmux, agent daemon, lock, inbox item, notification or event-log line, and nothing is written to the real run store. `status:` commands and `require:` proofs are never run: the script says how each ends. Time moves a second for each agent turn (so a file an earlier attempt left reads as stale, as in a real run) and when a retry's `backoff:` is waited out, so the same script prints the same transcript every time.

The fake agent acts when a step waits for it. It writes the step's `produces:` files (non-empty, as a strict run needs), reports if the script says so, and ends its turn, which is what makes the engine decide the step. At a gate a fake person approves or rejects. The simulation ends when the run is `completed`, `failed` or `cancelled`, when it waits for a person (outcome `stopped`: a `failed_check` once `on_fail` and `max_attempts` are used up, a spent token budget, a turn that ended without its files, or an engine refusal, which the history shows as `simulated_refused`), or when it has taken 200 actions (outcome `capped`, `capped: true`, and the text says so). A stop is the answer, not an error: the exit code is 0.

The script is YAML, at most 64 KiB, with two keys. `inputs:` gives values for the workflow's inputs (`--input` wins). `steps:` maps a step id to a list with one entry per attempt of that step, counting an `on_fail` loop's reruns and a rejected gate's:

```yaml
inputs: {task: add a hello script}
steps:
  verify:
    - {check: fail, log: "2 failures", tokens: 40000}   # attempt 1
    - {check: fail, tokens: 40000}                      # attempt 2
    - {}                                                # attempt 3 passes
```

| Key of an attempt | Meaning (default) |
|-------------------|-------------------|
| `writes` | The `produces:` files written (all of them) |
| `report`, `summary`, `code` | What `step done` says; `report: fail` fails the step at the turn's end (nothing reported) |
| `check` | How the step's `status:` check ends: `pass`, `fail`, `timeout` or `error` (`pass`); only for a step that has one |
| `log` | The check log's text, which `on_fail`'s `log_tail` reads |
| `require` | A map of the step's `require:` names to `pass` or `fail` (`pass`); a failing one fails the step with `require_NAME`. A step with several `exec` entries takes a list in order, `exec: [pass, fail]`, one answer per entry (a list of another length is refused as `invalid_script`); a bare `exec: fail` answers all of them |
| `gate`, `note` | `approve` or `reject` at the step's gate, and the note (`approve`) |
| `tokens` | The attempt's output tokens, counted against `budget:` (0) |
| `reviews` | For a step with `review:`: a map of its reviewers (`review.from`) to `approve` or `reject`, the verdicts the round's reviewers write. A reviewer the map leaves out writes nothing, so the round runs that reviewer again while the definition's `redeliver` lasts (see `reruns`), then stops `review_incomplete`; with no `reviews:` (or `{}`) no reviewer writes |
| `reruns` | For a step with `review:`: a list, one map like `reviews` per rerun of the round's missing reviewers, in order (the first for the first rerun); a rerun past the list writes nothing |
| `unchanged` | For a step with `review:`: `true` gives the attempt the same revision as the attempt before it (the author changed nothing), so a round after a rejected one stops `review_no_change` (`false`; each later attempt otherwise has a revision of its own) |
| `revision_changes` | For a step with `review:`: `true` makes the revision computed when the round ends differ from the one it was delivered with, so the round stops `review_stale` (`false`) |
| `pause` | `true`, or a reason text (1 to 4000 characters). Pauses the run while the attempt's step is `running`, before the agent's turn ends; the turn then ends while the run is paused. Without `unpause` the simulation stops there. A step waiting at a gate, in review or in backoff is never paused: the key is then ignored, without an error |
| `unpause` | `true`. Needs `pause` in the same entry. Unpauses once the turn has ended, which starts what the pause held |

`reviews`, `reruns`, `unchanged` and `revision_changes` belong to the author attempt being reviewed, not to a round: each round reviews one attempt, so entry N of a step is read by the round that reviews attempt N. A rejected round runs the step again as the next attempt (see [The rejection loop](#the-rejection-loop)), which takes the script's next entry, so `[{reviews: {skeptic: reject}}, {reviews: {skeptic: approve}}]` rejects attempt 1 and approves attempt 2 in round 2; the run stops `review_rejected` once `max_rounds` rounds reject.

```yaml
steps:
  design:
    - {reviews: {skeptic: reject, builder: approve}}   # the skeptic must approve, so the run stops review_rejected
```

A bad `reviews:` is refused as `invalid_script` with problems such as `steps.design attempt 1: reviews "nobody": the step has no such reviewer (reviewers: skeptic, builder)`, `reviews "skeptic" must be one of approve, reject, not "maybe"`, `reviews must be a mapping of the step's reviewers to approve or reject`, `reviews is set, and the step has no review:`, `revision_changes must be true or false`, `reruns must be a list, ...`, `reruns[0] "nobody": the step has no such reviewer ...` and `unchanged must be true or false`.

An attempt the list has no entry for does the plain thing. A script with a key, step, input, file or proof the workflow does not have, a bad value, a `code:` the engine sets itself (as `step done --code` refuses), or text that is not YAML is refused with `invalid_script`, listing every problem in `details.problems`. Problems for the pause keys read `pause must be true or a non-empty reason of at most 4000 characters (omit the key for no pause)`, `unpause must be true` and `unpause is set, and the attempt does not pause`. The composed instructions are the workflow's and the step's own text, without library packs: use `workflow render` for the real text.

Limits: programs of `require: exec:` entries are not looked up (the proofs do not run, so one that is not installed here does not stop the simulation) and `{{changed.tests}}` needs no `commands.test_files`; the checkout stub reads no repository, so a `guard:` can never fail and no `guard` failure can be scripted; `start`'s refusals for an unset `commands.test`/`lint` and for a first-step pack the library can't find are not made here (use `workflow validate`); locks are always free, so a `uses:` wait is not simulated; there is no daemon, so a sweep, a timeout or a lost turn is not either; a person's `resume` is not scripted, so a stop ends the simulation; a reviewed step's round is delivered at once with no review window, and every verdict the script gives is proven (the daemon's hook log is not read), so `review_unproven` and the other proof failures can't be scripted, and the checkout cannot change under a round (`review_guard`).

### Adding a definition

`workflow add ID PATH` (or `-` for stdin) checks the text as `validate --id ID` does, then writes it to `~/.config/workspace/workflows/ID.yml`. It writes nothing else: a preset is never touched, and a copy of a preset's id shadows it, which is how a preset is changed. An `id:` in the text must be ID or absent. A relative `prompt_file:` is read from `~/.config/workspace/workflows/`, where the file will be loaded from, so what passes is what `run` will load.

It is refused with `workflow_exists` when a file of that id is already there (a link counts, even one to a file that is gone); change it with `workflow update` instead. The check and the write happen under an exclusive lock on `.ID.yml.lock` in the same directory, so two adds of one id can't both pass; the text is written to a temp file there and renamed over `ID.yml`. The lock file is left in place. `--dry-run` takes the same lock and runs every check, and writes no definition; it may still create the workflows directory and the `.ID.yml.lock` file. An ID that could not be a file name is `invalid_workflow` before any path is touched. `add` writes no event-log line.

### Updating a definition

`workflow update ID PATH --if-sha256 SHA` (or `-` for stdin) checks the new text as `add` does (a relative `prompt_file:` is read from `~/.config/workspace/workflows/`), then replaces `~/.config/workspace/workflows/ID.yml` if the file's raw bytes still hash to SHA. Take SHA from `workflow show ID --source --json` or the rows of `workflow show --json`; both hash the raw bytes, valid or not. `--if-sha256` is the bare 64-hex sha256 (case and surrounding whitespace don't matter) and is required: without it `update` is a usage error.

The current file is read as bytes and never parsed, so a file that is invalid YAML, fails the checks or is not UTF-8 can still be replaced: read it with `show ID --source`, fix it, and update. Under the same exclusive lock `add` takes, `update` checks that the file exists (a link counts), compares the hashes and writes a temp file renamed over `ID.yml`, so a save that waited on another sees the other's bytes and fails. Outcomes:

- `updated` with the new `sha256`; `--dry-run` runs every check under the lock and answers `dry_run`, writing no definition.
- `stale_workflow` when the hashes differ (or the file can't be read): nothing is written and `details.sha256` is the current file's, so the editor can reload. Take the current text with `show ID --source` and merge.
- `builtin_workflow` when only a preset has the id: presets are never written; save a copy with `workflow add`.
- `unknown_workflow` when nothing has the id.

`update` writes no event-log line.

### Removing a definition

`workflow remove ID` deletes `~/.config/workspace/workflows/ID.yml`. Without `--yes` (and without `--dry-run`) it asks `Remove workflow ID (PATH)? [y/N]` first, before taking any lock; anything but `y` prints `Cancelled.`, answers nothing and exits 0. Under `--no-input` (or `WORKSPACE_NO_INPUT`, which is how workspace-ui calls it) it refuses with `confirmation_required` and the retry flag `--yes`; `--json` without `--yes` or `--dry-run` is a usage error, since it never prompts.

Under the exclusive lock `add` and `update` take, `remove` checks that the file exists (a link counts), then unlinks it. A link is removed itself and never its target; a file that is invalid, not UTF-8 or a link to nothing can be removed, which is the way out `stale_workflow` points to for an unreadable file. A directory at that path is refused with `invalid_workflow` and nothing is removed. The `.ID.yml.lock` file stays. Outcomes:

- `removed`; when a preset has the same id the message says so ("the preset of the same id is in effect again"). `--dry-run` runs the same checks under the lock and answers `dry_run`, deleting nothing.
- `builtin_workflow` when only a preset has the id: a preset can't be removed.
- `unknown_workflow` when nothing has the id.

`remove` writes no event-log line.

### Listing the workflows

`workflow list [--json]` prints one row per definition the lookup finds — id, where it came from (project, global, `source:N` or builtin) and its `description:` (else its title). A definition that can't be read says `INVALID` with its first problem. `--json` is the same list as one document.

### Installing from sources

`workflow install [--source N] [--json]` reads each entry of the global `workflows.sources` config key and makes its `.yml` definitions available to every command, below your own files and above the presets. An entry is a local directory (absolute or `~/`) or a GitHub directory (`https://github.com/OWNER/REPO/tree/REF/PATH` or `owner/repo/path`; `REF` is a branch, tag or commit and may hold slashes, such as `tree/me/topic/workflows`, which `install` settles by asking GitHub for each split in turn; read with `gh`, so `gh` must be installed and logged in; without it the source fails with `workflow_source_gh_missing`). Set the list with `workspace config set workflows.sources`, or with [`init --workflow-sources`](README.init.md#workflow-sources), which also installs the sources it adds. `install` is a global command: there is no `--name`.

Each file of each source is checked through the same lookup a run uses, so an `extends:` finds a preset or a sibling as it will later. A file that sets `prompt_file:` is refused (`workflow_source_prompt_file`): use `prompt:` instead. One bad file refuses its whole source, and the report lists each problem with its file; the cache keeps that source's previous version, and the other sources still install. A GitHub source is first staged beside its cache and swapped in only when everything passed, so a source is replaced whole or not at all. A local source is checked where it is and never copied. `install` writes only the cache and never `~/.config/workspace/workflows/`.

Run it again to update. The report names, per source, the workflows that are new (`installed`), `changed` or `removed` since the cached version, and warns (`shadowed`) when a source's definition hides a preset or an earlier source's definition of the same id, or is itself hidden by one of yours. Each definition shows its provenance in `workflow show` as `source:N`, the position in `workflows.sources`. `--source N` installs only the source at position N (1 is the first); write the number after the flag. With nothing listed, `install` says so and exits 0. `--json` prints one document: `outcome` (`installed`, `unchanged` or `failed`), a `sources` row each (`index`, `source`, `kind`, `path`, `outcome`, `installed`, `changed`, `removed`, `workflows`, `problems`) and `warnings`. A refused source makes the outcome `failed` and the exit code 1.

To stop using a source, remove it from `workflows.sources` and delete its cache directory, `<state>/.workflows-sources/<sha256-of-source>/`; the report prints that path for each GitHub source. `workflow uninstall [--yes] [--dry-run] [--json]` does the cache half in one move: it deletes every GitHub source's cache directory (so the presets and your own files in `~/.config/workspace/workflows/` are all that is found again), reports a local source without touching it (it is read in place, so there is nothing to remove), and never edits `workflows.sources` — the report says to unset the entries with `workspace config unset workflows.sources`. It asks before it deletes; `--yes` skips the question, `--dry-run` removes nothing and asks nothing. `--json` is one document with `outcome` (`uninstalled`, `dry_run`, `unchanged` or `failed`) and a `sources` row each. The outcome is `failed` (exit 1) when `workflows.sources` can't be read (`reason` `workflows_sources_unreadable`) or a cache directory survived the delete (`reason` `cache_not_removed`).

Other codes: `workflow_source_invalid` (an entry is not a usable source), `workflow_source_local_missing` (a local directory is not readable), `workflow_source_failed` (`gh` could not read the directory, or timed out).

### Reading a run's events

`workflow events RUN` prints the run's history file (`<RUN>.events.jsonl`, in `runs/` while the run is going and in `archive/` after), one line per event: the line number, the time, the type and the rest of the line's keys as `key=value`. It reads the file and writes nothing; a run with no history file has no events. An unknown run is refused with `unknown_run`. `--since N` must be a whole number, 0 or more, or it is a usage error.

Each event's `n` is its 1-based line number in the file, so a reader that remembers the last `n` (or `next_since`) asks for `--since N` and gets only what is new. A complete line that is not a JSON object (hand-edited, or blank: a blank line counts as a bad line) is skipped, keeps its number and is named in `warnings`. A last line with no newline yet is an event still being written: it is not returned, counted or warned about, and `next_since` stays at the last complete line, so the next poll gets it. At most 1000 events are answered; when more follow, `truncated` is true and `next_since` is the line number to pass as `--since`.

#### Following a run's events

`workflow events RUN --follow [--since N] [--types A,B] [--timeout DURATION] [--json]` prints the history from `--since` (default 0), then keeps reading and prints each event as it is appended. With `--json` each event is one JSON line (the same keys as in `events`, with no document around them). It reads the file every quarter of a second, and reads again at once while more than 1000 events wait. Once the run has finished, which it counts from the moment the run is moved to the archive (a finishing change writes the run's state before its last events, so the state alone is not enough), and its history is drained, it prints one last line and stops. To resume a later follow, pass the last event's `n` (or `follow_ended.next_since`) as `--since`; this is a line number, not the opaque `cursor` of `workspace events`. A line that is not a JSON object is skipped with a warning on stderr. If moving a finished run to the archive fails, `--follow` keeps waiting until a later change archives it. An unknown run is refused with `unknown_run`. The `workflow_events_follow` capability (revision 2) says the contract below exists.

With `--json` the stream is made of these lines, and `follow_started`, `gap` and `follow_ended` always print, whatever `--types` says (`workspace events` uses the same `follow_started` and `gap` names; see [its types](README.events.md)):

- `{"type":"follow_started","run","since","state","current","definition_sha256"}` first: the line number the follow resumes from and the run's state, current step and definition hash when it began.
- `{"type":"gap","at","run","reason","next_since"}` when lines were lost. `history_shorter`: the history now has fewer lines than the follow had read (the file was rewritten), so the follow carries on from `next_since`, the new last line. `not_retained`: the run's files were pruned while it was followed; a `follow_ended` with reason `pruned` comes next.
- `{"type":"follow_ended","run","state","reason","next_since"}` last. `reason` is `completed`, `cancelled`, `killed` (cancelled because its workspace was killed), `failed`, `pruned` (the archive removed the run; `state` is `finished` because the real one is unknown) or `timeout` (`state` is the run's state when the wait ended). Text output prints `Run RUN STATE; no more events.`, with the reason in parentheses when it differs from the state, a `Gap in RUN's history (REASON); continuing from line N.` line for a gap, and `Stopped following RUN after 30m; run is going.` for a timeout.

`--types A,B` (repeat it or comma-separate; also allowed on a plain `workflow events RUN`) prints only events of those types. `n` and `next_since` still count every line, so resuming with `--since` neither repeats nor skips an event.

`--timeout DURATION` (`30s`, `5m`, `1h`; only with `--follow`, else a usage error) stops waiting that long after the follow began, even in the middle of a long backlog, unless the run has already finished, in which case the follow ends with the run's own reason.

Exit codes of `--follow`: 0 when the run completed or was pruned, and on Ctrl-C or a closed pipe; 1 when it ended `cancelled`, `killed` or `failed`; 75 when `--timeout` ran out while the run was still going. A cancelled run used to exit 0 here, so a script that relied on that must now read 1; `state` and `reason` still tell the cases apart. A plain read without `--follow` exits 0.

Limits: a reason `workspace_removed` (the run's workspace was removed) is not reported. The acceptance item asking for it was narrowed, not met: nothing records a workspace removed other than by `kill`, so a run left going is ended by `--timeout`. A follow also cannot see a history that a cut-short archive finished by a later append moved to different line numbers without making the file shorter: no `gap` is printed for it. When a cut-short archive leaves a new, shorter live history, the follow prints a `history_shorter` gap and may print some lines a second time once the archive is finished. A `--since` past the end of the history is reported as a `history_shorter` gap too. A run pruned in the moment between the follow seeing it archived and reading its history ends with reason `pruned` and no `not_retained` gap. A run that finishes in the same poll the deadline passes, after the follow checked it, can end with reason `timeout` and exit 75 while `state` already says how it finished.

#### Keys that say why a run changed

`workflow events --json` lines written by a run change carry five more keys: `from_state`, `to_state`, `actor`, `cause` and, on `step_dispatching`, `retry_of`. They came with the `workflow` capability at revision 41 (see `workspace capabilities --json`, and [`capabilities`](README.capabilities.md)). For example, the line written when a review round rejected a step and sent it back for another attempt (the reviewers ended their turn, so the daemon's `workflow advance` made the change):

```json
{"n":12,"ts":"2026-10-25T09:31:07.482Z","type":"step_dispatching","step":"research","attempt":2,"retry_of":1,"from_state":"running","to_state":"running","actor":"daemon","cause":"review_rejected"}
```

A run stored before that revision has none of the five keys on its old lines, so a reader treats a missing key as `null`. Existing keys keep their names and types. `ts` is not new, but it now has milliseconds (`2026-10-25T09:30:00.123Z`).

- `from_state` and `to_state` are the run's `state` (`waiting`, `running`, `completed`, `failed` or `cancelled`) before and after the change that wrote the line. Every line one change writes shows the same pair: where the run was when the change began and where it was when it ended. The state is the stored one with nothing hidden, so a run that waits at a gate, waits for a lock and waits out a retry backoff reads `running` to `waiting` on the line that starts each of the three waits, and `waiting` to `running` on the line that ends it; [Telling a waiting run's reason apart](#telling-a-waiting-runs-reason-apart) shows how to tell them. A run whose agent ended its turn while its background work is still running reads `running` to `running`.
- A `from_state` and `to_state` of `null` on a line that has the keys means the line was written after the change was stored and its lock was let go, so it has no pair. Two lines are written this way: `step_dispatched` (actor `engine`, and the cause that started the attempt) and the `step_recovered` a redelivery writes (actor `sweep`, cause `redelivery`).
- A missing key, with no `null` written, is read as `null`. Every line of a run stored before revision 41 lacks the keys, and a few lines of a new run lack them too: `command_denied` and other lines written outside any run change. `workspace capabilities --json` shows whether this CLI has the revision.
- `actor` is one of five values: `person`, `agent`, `daemon`, `sweep` or `engine`. It is who caused the change. `person` is a `workflow` command someone ran; `agent` is `step done` from the run's pane; `daemon` is `workflow advance` and the agent daemon's timers; `sweep` is `workflow sweep`. A line that follows from a change, such as the result of a step's `status:` check or the end of a review round, has the actor of the change that led to it. `engine` is only for work the engine does on its own: recording whether the prompt typed into the pane arrived, recording a review round's delivery, running a review round again, and writing `step_dispatched`. `person` is what the caller says, not a checked identity: a check refuses approve and reject from the run's own pane, but nothing proves who is at the keyboard.
- `cause` is a short code with two meanings. A line that has a `cause` of its own keeps it: `step_failed` keeps its failure kind (`check`, `guard`, `timeout` and the like), and the `step_stalled`, `step_recovered` and `run_cancelled` lines keep theirs. Every other line gets the cause of the change that wrote it.
- `retry_of` on `step_dispatching` is the attempt's own `retry_of`. It is set only when a rejected review round started the attempt; a `loop` or backoff retry reads `null`, and `cause` tells them apart.

The run is stored first and its lines are written after, while the run's lock is still held. Lines are therefore in the order the changes happened, and one change's `to_state` is the next change's `from_state`. A change that fails stores nothing and writes no lines. A crash after the run is stored and before its lines are written leaves the stored change with no lines; the run file is the record, so a reader that checks it is never misled by a line for a change that did not happen.

##### Telling a waiting run's reason apart

Because `running` to `waiting` is the same for every wait, read the event type first and the `cause` second.

| The run waits for | Starts with | Ends with |
|---|---|---|
| A person at a gate | `gate_waiting` | `gate_approved`, or `gate_rejected` followed by `step_dispatching` (cause `gate_rejected`) |
| A lock a step `uses:` | no line in the run's history. `lock_wait_started` and `lock_acquired` are in the [event log](README.event-log.md), which `workspace events` reads; match them by `run_id` | `step_dispatching`, carrying the cause the attempt had before it waited. If none was kept (a run stored before causes were kept), the cause is `lock_ended` |
| A retry backoff | `step_failed`, then `step_looped` with a `backoff` key (the wait, such as `30s`); the run is waiting and there is no `gate_waiting` line | `step_retry_started`, or `step_retry_blocked` when the pane is no longer the run's; the retry's `step_dispatching` has cause `backoff_ended` (or `resume` when a person did not wait) |

##### The causes

The cause a change gives its lines (the second meaning above: lines with no `cause` of their own):

| Cause | The change |
|---|---|
| `start` | the run was started |
| `approve`, `reject` | a person decided a gate |
| `resume` | a person resumed the run (also `resume --restart-step`, whose lines are `step_interrupted`, `run_resumed` with `restart_step: true`, and `step_dispatching` with `retry_of`) |
| `park` | a resume was stored for later because the pane was busy (the run is "parked") |
| `release_park` | the pane went idle and the stored resume ran |
| `park_failed` | the stored resume could not run |
| `cancel`, `kill` | a person cancelled the run; the workspace was killed |
| `turn_ended` | an agent's turn ended and the engine decided the step |
| `report` | the agent reported on its step with `step done` |
| `delivery` | the engine recorded whether the prompt typed into the pane arrived |
| `tick` | a timer pass (the daemon's, or the sweep's) moved a run that could move: a backoff ended, a lock came free, a holder died, or a review round was due. When a backoff ended, the retry's `step_dispatching` has cause `backoff_ended` and its other lines `tick` |
| `backoff_ended` | the sweep started a retry whose backoff had ended; every line of that change has this cause |
| `redelivery` | the sweep typed an undelivered prompt into the pane again |
| `infra_retry` | the sweep or `workflow advance` scheduled, claimed, released or gave up an [automatic restart](#automatic-restarts) (the restart's own `resume` lines have cause `resume`) |
| `review_round` | a review round was delivered or its window was handled |
| `round_rerun` | a review round ran again for the reviewers whose verdicts did not count |
| `timeout` | a step ran past its `timeout:` and failed |
| `timed_out` | the sweep pressed the interrupt keys (the keys that stop an agent's turn) because a step passed its `timeout:` |
| `run_timed_out` | the sweep pressed the interrupt keys because the run passed its `timeout:` |

A sweep that notices something wrong writes a `step_stalled` or `step_recovered` line with a cause of its own, which stays on the line (see [Sweeping stopped runs](#sweeping-stopped-runs)). `workflow sweep` rows use other codes of their own; only these appear on run lines:

| Cause | What the sweep found |
|---|---|
| `pane_gone`, `agent_gone`, `daemon_down` | the run's pane, its agent or its agent daemon is gone |
| `server_restarted` | the tmux server a review round's pane was on was restarted (review rounds only) |
| `idle_turn_over` | the agent's turn ended and nothing decided the step |
| `turn_failed` | the agent's turn ended on an API error and is not coming back: recovery gave up, or the failure is older than `workflows.turn_failed_after` |
| `undelivered` | a prompt never reached the pane (the `step_stalled` of a redelivery change, whose other lines get `redelivery`) |
| `redelivery_exhausted` | the prompt was typed again as often as `redeliver` allows and still did not arrive |
| `review_timed_out` | a review round passed its `review.timeout` |
| `interrupted` | the sweep pressed the interrupt keys (for a step or a review round) and the turn then ended |
| `interrupt_unanswered` | keys were pressed for every time limit due and the review pane still did not stop |

The causes that start an attempt are written on `step_dispatching` and `step_dispatched`: `first` (the run's first step), `next_step` (the step after a pass), `loop` (a `loop` retry), `backoff_ended`, `review_rejected` (a rejected review round), `resume`, `gate_rejected` (a rejected gate), `redelivery` and `lock_ended`. An attempt that has to wait for a `uses:` lock writes its own cause once the wait ends, as the table above says.

### Produced files

A run started with this version stores `"produces_check": "strict"` in its definition snapshot (`workflow status --json` does not show it; the run file does). For such a run each file a step `produces` must be, at the turn's end, a regular file (read with `lstat`, so a symlink as the last part of the path is never followed, even one to a real file; a symlinked directory earlier in the path is followed, as the check does not look at the parents) that is not empty and was written during this attempt. A symlink, a directory or an empty file keeps the step from passing: the run stores `turn_ended_incomplete` with `details.invalid`, a list of `{file, rule}` where `rule` is `symlink`, `not_regular_file` or `empty`. The run holds there: `step status` and `workflow status` name the rule that was broken. Nothing is typed to the agent; the next turn's end or `workflow resume` looks again. A link to nothing is a `symlink`, not `missing`. This is a hold, not a failure, so there is no `details.code` or `code_source`, and no `on_fail` applies. A run stored before this change has no marker and keeps the old check: the path exists (through a link, a directory counts) and was written this attempt. `step status` and the `turn_ended` decision use the same check.

### What an attempt records

Each attempt in the run file (`~/.local/state/workspace/.workflows/runs/<RUN>.json`, under `steps.<id>.attempts`) keeps, besides its number, times, prompt file, report and outcome, a record of what it started from and ended with. `workflow status --json` shows only the count of attempts, so read the run file for these:

| Key | Set | Meaning |
|-----|-----|---------|
| `head.before` | when the attempt begins | what `git rev-parse HEAD` answers in the run's checkout path, or `null` (unborn branch, git could not answer, or git took longer than 5 seconds). A checkout that lost its `.git` inside another repository reads that repository's HEAD. A `require: commits` check counts from this commit. |
| `head.after` | when the attempt ends (see below) | HEAD then, read the same way, or `null` |
| `remote` | before the attempt's line is typed, only for a step that requires `remote_unchanged` | `{remote, ref, sha}`: the branch on its remote, from `git ls-remote`; or `{unread}` with why there is no reading (`detached_head`, `local_upstream`, `bad_remote`, `no_remote`, `unreachable`); absent for a step that does not require it |
| `proofs` | as each `require:` proof that runs outside the run's lock goes (`command`, `remote_unchanged`, `exec`) | one row per proof run outside the lock, and one per skipped optional `command`: `proof`, `index` (its place in `require:`), `timeout`, `started_at`, `ended_at`, `token`; for `command` also `role`, `command`, `log`, `exit_code`, `timed_out`, `error`; an optional `command` whose project command is unset leaves a row with `skipped: "command_unset"`, `optional: true`, `role`, `index`, `started_at` and `ended_at` (and no token); for `remote_unchanged` also `before` and the reading; for `exec` also `program` (the first word as written, not the filled-in one), `log`, `exit_code`, `timed_out` and `error`, never the arguments. Absent for a step with neither |
| `definition_sha256` | when the attempt begins | the run's definition hash (the run's own `definition_sha256`) |
| `prompt_sha256` | when the attempt begins | SHA-256 of the prompt file as written |
| `packs` | when the attempt begins | `[{ref, sha256}]`, each pack composed into the prompt |
| `outputs` | when the attempt ends (see below) | `[{file, sha256, bytes}]`, one per `produces` file, named as the definition names it; `[]` for a step with no `produces`. `sha256` and `bytes` are `null` for a file that is missing, a symlink, not a regular file or unreadable. A file above 16 MiB is not read: `bytes` is its size, `sha256` is `null` and `too_large` is `true`. A link as the last part of the path is never followed; a linked parent directory is, as in the `produces` check above. |
| `evidence` | the prompt when the attempt begins, the rest when it ends (see below) | `[{name, kind, source, sha256, bytes}]`: the attempt's prompt (`kind` `prompt`, copied as it is written, before it is delivered), check log (`check_log`, only when it had a check), each `produces` file (`output`) and each verdict file a review round read (`review`, added as the round is recorded, after what is there). The copy is `~/.local/state/workspace/.workflows/evidence/<RUN>/<name>` (`evidence_dir/<id>/<name>`); `source` is the path it was copied from. `name` is `<step>.<attempt>.prompt.md`, `<step>.<attempt>.check.log`, `<step>.<attempt>.out.<index>.<file>` or `<step>.<attempt>.r<round>.review.<reviewer>.md`, where `<index>` is the file's place in `produces` and anything outside letters, digits, `.`, `_` and `-` in the file becomes `_`, so two files never share a name. `sha256` is the hash of the bytes copied. Nothing is copied, and `sha256` is `null`, for a file with `problem` `missing` (missing, a link, not a regular file or unreadable), `outside_artifacts` (its directory, links resolved, is not inside the run's artifacts directory) or `copy_failed` (the copy could not be written); one above 16 MiB has `too_large` `true` and its size, and `budget` `true` as well when it fit the limit but the attempt's files together passed 64 MiB (a review round's verdicts have a 64 MiB budget of their own, per round). A problem never fails the step; `null` for an attempt stored before this key existed. See [Evidence outside the checkout](#evidence-outside-the-checkout). The `review` kind needs `workflow` 33. |
| `code` | when the attempt fails or is not delivered | the failure's `code` (`require_<proof>`, a guard code, `check_failed`, or the agent's own code from `step done --code`); for a `not_delivered` attempt the delivery's code (`not_delivered`, `pane_gone`, `no_such_pane`, ...). Absent for an attempt that passed, was cancelled, or was stored before this key existed. It stays on the attempt when a retry begins; the retry has its own |
| `check.command` | when the check starts | the command that ran, as resolved then: a later change to `commands.test` does not rewrite it. Absent when the project's command is unset. A step that names `status: {run: ...}` also has that text in the run's stored definition; one that names `command: test` or `lint` has only the role there, so this key is the only record of the text that ran. |
| `session` | when the attempt ends | `{id, transcript}`: the latest coding-agent session started in the run's pane at or before the attempt's end, from the session ledger. Only entries recorded under the run's workspace (or its tmux session's name) and, when both sides know it, the same tmux server count, since tmux reuses pane ids. A session that ended before the attempt began is not chosen. Only a SessionEnd at or after the start closes it, so a session resumed under the same id counts as live. One that began before the attempt (more than 5 seconds before) and never ended is chosen when the pane's tmux server is known and is the one it started under; otherwise only when its transcript has a line inside the attempt's window, and with no way to prove that the key is `null`. The server is read with a 2 second limit; no answer (also what a pane that is gone gives) proves nothing. A fresh-conversation restart or a `/clear` starts a session after the attempt begins, so the last one is the conversation that did the work. `id` must be letters, digits, `.`, `_`, `-`; `transcript` must be an absolute `.jsonl` path with no control characters, else `null`. Only the last 1 MiB of the ledger is read, and lines are matched on the pane id before they are parsed |
| `agent` | when the attempt ends | `{kind, version, model}`: `kind` from the ledger (`claude` when it names none); `version` and `model` from the transcript's lines inside the attempt's time window, each `null` when not read or not of the expected shape. `null` with `session` |
| `usage` | when the attempt ends | `{input_tokens, output_tokens}` plus `cache_creation_input_tokens` and `cache_read_input_tokens` when not zero. It is the main thread's count plus each in-step sub-agent's: Claude Code writes a sub-agent to its own file, `<transcript dir>/<session id>/subagents/agent-<id>.jsonl`, and every such file is read with the same window and tail rules and added in (the path is derived from the session's transcript path, never taken from a hook payload, and no link is followed; a link, a session id that is not the transcript file's own name, more than 4096 directory entries, or more than 64 `agent-*.jsonl` files last written since the attempt began makes `usage` `null` (older files are skipped before the count, so a long session does not lose its usage for good); a file with nothing in the window adds nothing; a file whose last 4 MiB does not reach the attempt's start, or with a usage line without a readable time, makes it `null`). Main-transcript `isSidechain` lines are still left out. Tokens written after `ended_at` are not counted (`step done` stamps `ended_at` mid-turn, so later tokens of that turn are left out). It sums the Claude Code transcript's assistant messages whose first line is stamped between the attempt's `started_at` and `ended_at` (a cancelled attempt counts to the time of the cancel), each message by its last line. A resumed attempt in the same session therefore does not count earlier attempts. `null`, never a smaller number, for another agent; for a transcript that is missing, a link, not a regular file or without a message in the window; when the last 4 MiB read (the limit, since this runs under the run's lock) does not reach back to the attempt's start, or, for a session whose `source` is `startup` or `clear` (which begin a new transcript file; `resume` and `compact` keep the old one), to that session's first line when that began later; when any usage line in the tail has no readable time; when a message's `usage` is present but not an object, and when a count is present but not a whole number from 0 up (an absent count is 0) |
| `warnings` | when the guard warns | `[{code, at, message}]`: what the guard warned of while this attempt was the one checked (`config_changed`, see [A changed config](#a-changed-config)). Absent when there was none |
| `reasons` | on every change of the run's reason | `[{code, since, until[, parked]}]`: each reason the run showed while this attempt owned it, oldest first; `until` is `null` for the one still standing. A resume parked for a busy pane is recorded as the reason `parked`; a park that times out is worked out when read and never stored, so its entry stays open until a change drops the park. `parked: true` is transient, present only while a park stands over the wait it closed. A change of code or `since` closes the open entry and opens another. An open entry with the same code and `since` is never repeated, wherever it sits, so a reason that stands when a later attempt opens stays on the attempt it began on. A new entry goes on the step's open attempt; else on the attempt that change just ended, only when its ending set the reason (a failed check for that attempt, a gate wait); else on the run (below). The reason `create` sets (`not_started`) is recorded too |
| `rounds` | as each review round of a step with a `review` ends | `[{round, pane, started_at, ended_at, session, agent, usage[, packs], ended[, reviews]}]`, oldest first: one record for every review round that ended on this attempt, and the one list a round's usage is read from. `round` is the round's number; `pane` the review pane (`null` until a round records one); `started_at` and `ended_at` its time window; `session`, `agent` and `usage` are read as for the attempt, but from the review pane over the round's window, and each is `null` when it can't be told (always for a round with no `pane`). `ended` is `approved`, `rejected` or `incomplete` for a round its verdicts decided, `stale` (its revision changed while it ran, see [A review round's revision](#a-review-rounds-revision)), `review_guard` (the checkout changed while it ran, see [A review round's guard](#a-review-rounds-guard)), `resumed` (a person's resume ended a stopped round and started a fresh one), `from` (`resume --from` ended it), `cancelled` (the run was cancelled or its workspace killed), `review_tampered` or `review_incomplete` (the round's directory could not be made for it, see [Verdict files](#verdict-files); the round never ran), `redelivered` (a delivery that failed or lapsed, recorded before the same round is delivered again with a new `started_at`, so the time and usage of the lapsed delivery are kept), or `rerun` (the part of a round before only its missing reviewers ran again, see [An unchanged revision and missing verdicts](#an-unchanged-revision-and-missing-verdicts); needs `workflow` 38). `reviews` is present only when the round read verdicts: the rows it read (see [Verdict files](#verdict-files)), each with `isolation` `review_pane` (the verdict was written by a sub-agent of the round's own review pane), so a later reader can tell these verdicts from any other kind; the attempt's own `reviews` (on a decided round) carries the same rows. The verdict files it read are copied into the attempt's `evidence` (kind `review`). `packs` is present once the round was delivered: the reviewer packs its prompt composed in, one `{reviewer, ref, sha256}` each (see [A review round's window](#a-review-rounds-window)). Absent on an attempt no round ended on. Needs `workflow` 33. |
| `retry_of` | when the attempt begins, only for an attempt a review round's rejection started | the `n` of the attempt the round rejected (see [The rejection loop](#the-rejection-loop)); `workflow attempts --json` shows it, `null` for any other attempt. Needs `workflow` 36. |

Which outcomes have end fields (`head.after`, `outputs`):

| How the attempt ended | End fields |
|-----------------------|------------|
| `passed` | yes |
| `failed` (a check that exited non-zero, timed out or could not run, a guard, an agent's report of failure) | yes |
| the run was cancelled, or its workspace was killed, while the attempt was open | yes, and the attempt is closed: `ended_at` is set and `outcome` is `cancelled`; a `check` that was running keeps no `ended_at` or `exit_code`, and unfinished `proofs` rows stay as they were, since the attempt's own `outcome` says it ended (counted as `unfinished` by `workflow stats`, not a miss in `workflow compare`); an attempt that already ended (for example one waiting at a gate) is left as it was |
| `not_delivered` (nothing ran) | no, except the prompt's `evidence` entry, copied before delivery |
| `interrupted` (the agent crashed or its pane went, and `resume --restart-step` closed the attempt) | yes, and the attempt is closed: `ended_at` is set and `outcome` is `interrupted`; `usage` is read from the old pane's session, once |
| a parked resume or a background hold that ran out | no attempt ends, so none |

In every end field `null` means git or the file could not answer: a removed checkout gives a `null` `head.after`, and a removed artifacts directory gives `outputs` with `null` hashes. A recording problem never fails the step, the cancel or the kill. A reason set when the step has no attempt to own it (a wait for a lock before the first attempt, or before a retry's attempt exists) is kept in the run's own `reasons` list, with a `step` key naming the step; the same list shape applies. That includes a dispatch that could not start, and a wait for a lock on an `on_fail` retry. The run's list holds the waits outside any attempt, so a reader joins them to attempts by `step` and by time (`since`/`until`). A wait that a parked resume interrupts and the park gives back stays one entry: the park closes it with a transient `parked: true` mark, and the entry is reopened (`until` cleared, mark removed) when the park ends with the same wait standing. `session`, `agent` and `usage` are added with the end fields (`head.after`, `outputs`), including for a cancelled or killed run's open attempt. An attempt stored before these keys existed has none of them (every new key is optional), and `head.after` and `outputs` are only added when it ends.

### Evidence outside the checkout

The prompt, the check log and the `produces` files live in the checkout, where the agent can edit them and `git worktree remove` deletes them. The engine copies them to `~/.local/state/workspace/.workflows/evidence/<RUN>/` and records each copy's name and SHA-256 in the attempt's `evidence`. The prompt is copied when it is written, before it is delivered, so an attempt that was never delivered still has it. The check log and the outputs are copied when the attempt ends (including a cancelled or killed run's open attempt). A review round's verdict files (`.workflow/<RUN>/reviews/<step>.<attempt>.r<round>/<reviewer>.md`) are copied when the round is recorded, decided or ended by a person, and each entry is added to the reviewed attempt's `evidence`; only a verdict the round read (one with a `sha256` in its row) is copied, under the same rules as the other files. The copy reopens the file, so a verdict rewritten after the round read it is still copied, and its entry gets `problem` `changed_since_read` with the hash the round read as `read_sha256` beside the copy's `sha256`; `workflow audit` reports it as a mismatch. `kill`, `finish` and `cancel` leave the directory alone. It goes when the run's archive entry is pruned past the newest 200 finished runs (the evidence first, then the archive files); a run still going is never pruned.

Each file must lie under the run's artifacts directory once links in its parent directories are resolved (else `outside_artifacts`), is opened without following a link, taken only if it is a regular file, and hashed and copied from that one handle. `outputs` takes its hash from the same copy when evidence is recorded, so one attempt never holds two hashes of one file. One attempt copies at most 64 MiB in all; the limit is per attempt, so a run's evidence grows with its attempts, up to the archive's retention. A file the copy did not take is still hashed for `outputs` as before, except one refused as `outside_artifacts`, which `outputs` records with a `null` hash. The file is opened by its resolved path and checked against a fresh resolution after opening, so a directory swapped for a link in between gives `outside_artifacts`. A stray temp file (`.tmp.<pid>.<name>`) from a copy that died is swept at the next copy, when its process is gone or it is over an hour old.

An agent under the same account can still change a file before it is copied, or the copy itself. The evidence makes a later change visible by comparing against the copy and its hash; it does not prevent one, and the prompt is the only file copied before the agent could touch it. A check log is whole when a check passes or fails; a cancel or kill that stops a running check records the log as it stood, and what the stopped check writes afterwards is not copied. A run stored before this has no `evidence` (`null` in `workflow attempts`).

### Token budgets

`budget: {tokens: 4000000, action: stop}` (or `action: interrupt`) holds a run to a token total across all its steps. It is off unless a definition declares it; a run stored before the key, or with a bad stored value, has no budget. `action` is `stop` or `interrupt`, and `stop` is the default. With `stop` the budget is checked where a new attempt would start and never interrupts a turn under way, so a run can finish an attempt past its budget: the budget stops the next one.

`action: interrupt` adds a press. The sweep reads the running attempt's tokens from its transcript (the live figure, below) and, when the stored spend plus the live figure reaches the allowance, presses the agent's interrupt keys once for the attempt, claimed, pressed and confirmed as for a step timeout, under its own cause and mark (`budget_interrupt`). It never fails the step or cancels the run: the turn ends, the step is decided as usual, and the next attempt's start holds the run as `budget_spent`, as `stop` does. `workflow resume --extra-tokens N` continues. Two sweeps look for it: the full sweep every 5 minutes, and `workflow sweep --budget-only` once a minute from the workspace's agent daemon while a run with such a budget is going. A budget-only pass reads only runs with an `interrupt` budget on a running step, and leaves every other run out of its rows. The press is recorded in `step_stalled` with the cause `budget_spent` (`used`, `allowance`, `live`), then `step_recovered` with the action `interrupted`.

The live figure. It counts the running attempt, the one `used` leaves out until it ends, from its transcript by the same rules as the stored `usage`: input, output and cache creation tokens, sub-agents included, cache reads left out. It is `null` when it can't be attributed to the attempt (another agent than Claude Code, an unreadable transcript, a session that can't be matched), and a `null` never trips the press. A `/clear` in the middle of an attempt counts only the new session. `workflow status` reads at most 4 MiB of transcript per run per call and keeps a small derived cache under `<runs dir>/live`; it is only a cache, so deleting it costs re-reading.

An attempt the press interrupted may end with no readable `usage` when its transcript is too long for the last 4 MiB to reach back to its start. It then counts the live figure the sweep saw, stored on the attempt as `budget_interrupt` (`tokens`, `used`, `allowance`, `at`), so the next attempt is still held. These attempts are counted in `budget.estimated`, as well as in `budget.counted`. A readable `usage` always wins over the record. `workflow stats` and `compare` stay on measured `usage` and never read the record.

In the text status the run reads:

```console
  budget_spent: step build was not started: 4,120,000 of 4,000,000 tokens  (resume --extra-tokens 1000000)
    workspace workflow resume wr_2611... --extra-tokens EXTRA_TOKENS
```

The figure after `resume --extra-tokens` is `suggested`; with uncounted attempts or review rounds the line adds `(at least; N ended attempts or review rounds not counted)`.

What is counted. The sum of the `usage` each ended attempt recorded ([What an attempt records](#what-an-attempt-records)): `input_tokens`, `output_tokens` and `cache_creation_input_tokens`. Cache reads are left out: they re-read context already paid for, and counting them would make any long turn look huge. In-step sub-agents are counted, since an attempt's `usage` includes each one's own transcript, so a budget on a step that runs sub-agents (the reviewers of `rpiv-auto-approve`, say) is spent faster than before they were counted. Each review round counts too: every record in an attempt's `rounds` (decided, ended by a person, or a lapsed delivery) adds its own `usage`, the review pane's session with its reviewer sub-agents over the round's window. The verdict rows carry no usage of their own, so nothing is counted twice; a round whose `usage` is `null` counts as one more in `budget.uncounted`. A round with no pane (one refused before it ran, or a delivery that lapsed before any pane opened) ran no agent: it adds no tokens and is neither counted nor uncounted. An attempt whose `usage` is `null` is not counted, so the total is always a **minimum**, and `status` says so (`budget.minimum` is always `true`). Two kinds of attempt need care:

- An attempt that was replaced without ending (a `resume --from` or `--pane` over a running attempt) counts with its `usage` when it has one, and as uncounted when it has none. The attempt a run is working on right now is left out until it ends, and is not uncounted. An `interrupted` attempt counts once, with the `usage` read when `resume --restart-step` closed it (`step_interrupted` says in `usage_counted` whether one was read); the attempt that restarts the step has its own usage.
- An attempt whose `usage` is `null`: another agent than Claude Code, or a heavy attempt whose last 4 MiB of transcript does not reach back to its start. These are counted in `budget.uncounted`, never read as zero. An attempt that was never delivered ran nothing and is neither counted nor uncounted.

Claude Code is the only agent measured. `budget.counted` and `budget.uncounted` count ended attempts and review rounds, each with its own `usage` (a round with no pane ran nothing and is in neither). A run whose ended attempts and rounds are all uncounted (a Codex run, say) has a total of 0, which is under any budget, so its budget never stops it; `budget.measured` is `false` and `budget.counted` is 0. The alternative, stopping on an unknown figure, would end every such run after its first attempt for a number nobody can check. A run with some counted and some uncounted attempts or rounds stops as soon as the counted ones alone reach the budget, since the real total is at least that.

Where it stops. A run is held when the budget is used (`used` has reached `tokens` plus `extra_tokens`) at each point an attempt would start:

| Where an attempt starts | With the budget used |
|---|---|
| A step passes and the next one starts, an `approve` | Held: the run waits, `budget_spent`, with the next step left as it was |
| An `on_fail` retry straight away | Held: the loop count is spent, the failure text and `context: fresh` are kept for the resume |
| An `on_fail` retry after its `backoff` (a tick or `workflow sweep`) | Held when the wait is over |
| `workflow resume`, `resume --from`, `resume --restart-step`, a `--pane` resume of a running step, `workflow reject`, a resume over a retry's wait | Refused with `budget_spent` unless `--extra-tokens N` covers it; nothing changes |
| A resume parked from the run's own pane | Refused when it is parked and when it is released; not when it is typed again after a busy pane (that attempt was already counted) |
| `workflow sweep` typing an undelivered line again | Not held: the attempt spent nothing new |
| A lock wait that ends | Not held: the attempt was allowed when the step was reached |
| `unchanged` answers, a running check, a turn left to decide | Not an attempt, never refused |
| A review round starts (the first round of a step with a `review`, or a fresh one on a resume after a stopped round) | Held: the step stays `reviewing` (see [A held review round](#a-held-review-round)) |
| A review round delivered again after its delivery failed or lapsed, or a round leaving a lock wait | Not held, as for an attempt |

A held run reads `waiting` with the reason `budget_spent` (`details`: `step`, `used`, `allowance`, `tokens`, `extra_tokens`, `counted`, `uncounted` (ended attempts and review rounds with and without a readable `usage`), `needed`, `suggested`), holds no lock, and keeps its pane binding. It is listed by `workflow status --needs-me` and has an inbox row (`workflow_budget_spent`). A hold sends the `failed` alert of `alerts.workflow` (once, when the hold begins; `WORKSPACE_ALERT_REASON` is `budget_spent` and the text says the token budget is used), since no sweep or status look would tell an unattended run's owner. It keeps the pane it had (`budget_hold` in the run file holds the pane and its identity, and the pane stays claimed against other runs like a retry's wait), so `resume --extra-tokens` is held with a sentence, writing nothing, when that pane is now another run's, gone, or looks reused; `resume --pane PANE` is the proof, as for a retry's wait. `resume --from STEP` drops the text that sent the run back to the held step, as it does for a retry's wait. `workflow sweep` reports a held run as `nothing` with the reason `budget_spent` (a hold is not a failure, so the exit status is 0).

`--extra-tokens N` (a whole number from 1 up) on `resume` and `reject` adds N to the run's allowance and goes on. N must cover the overrun (`used` minus the allowance plus one, `details.needed`, also in the message); `details.suggested` rounds that up to the next 100,000 so one more attempt has room. The run file keeps the total as `extra_tokens`, the event log records `budget_extended`, and the definition is not rewritten. The flag also applies when the budget is not used yet, and is ignored by a resume that starts no attempt and by a run without a budget. The event log records `budget_spent` when a run is held.

#### Verdict files

Needs `workflow` 33 in `capabilities` (reviewers, a step's `review`, the `reviewing` state, `rounds`, verdict rows with `isolation`, and verdict copies in `evidence`).

Each review round has its own directory, `<artifacts>/reviews/<step>.<attempt>.r<round>/`, which the engine makes as the round starts; anything already at that path stops the run with `review_tampered`. Each reviewer writes `<reviewer>.md` directly in it, starting with three lines in this order, then free text:

```
reviewer: skeptic
verdict: approve
revision: <the revision the round names>
```

`verdict` is `approve` or `reject`, in any case; a leading UTF-8 byte order mark is skipped, and the rest is read exactly as written. A reviewer may not be named `summary`, the engine's own `summary.md`. When the review pane's turn ends, a verdict counts only when all of these hold: it is a non-empty regular file, not a link (nor is `reviews/` or the round's directory); its header names its own reviewer, a known verdict and the round's revision; in the agent daemon's hook event log, the last successful `Write`, `Edit` or `MultiEdit` of the file came from the review pane, from a sub-agent (an `agent_id`), and carried the hash of the file as it is now (a later edit by any other means leaves the hash different), with no hook line lost after it; and no other counted verdict of the round came from the same sub-agent. The round is approved once the counted approvals reach `need` and include every `must`, and rejected (`review_rejected`) once the verdicts not counted could no longer change that; otherwise it is `review_incomplete`. A missing or broken verdict is never a rejection.

Not proven: that each sub-agent wrote only its own reviewer's file. A sub-agent of the review pane can write another reviewer's file with that reviewer's header, and it counts under its own `agent_id` (only two files from one `agent_id` are refused). Nor that the hook lines are genuine: `session-event` reads JSON on stdin, so a process run with `TMUX_PANE` set to the review pane can send a made-up PostToolUse with a matching hash, and it is accepted.

Each row of `reviews` holds `reviewer`, `path` (under the run's directory), `verdict` and `revision` (as the file said, `null` when unread), `agent_id`, `sha256` (of the bytes read), `counted`, and `problem` (`null` for a counted verdict, else the first rule it broke: `missing`, `symlink`, `not_regular_file`, `empty`, `too_large`, `unreadable`, `bad_header`, `wrong_reviewer`, `bad_verdict`, `wrong_revision`, `no_log`, `outside_checkout`, `no_write`, `log_gap`, `other_pane`, `main_thread`, `no_hash`, `hash_mismatch`, `changed`, `same_agent`, or for the whole round `no_round_dir`, `no_review_pane`, `no_revision`, `round_dir_missing`, `round_dir_link`). `review_outcome.approvals` is the number of counted approvals.

#### The rejection loop

A round that rejects the step ends the reviewed attempt `failed` (code `review_rejected`, with `reviews` and `review_outcome`) and runs the same step again as a new attempt, never through `on_fail` (which stays for the step's own checks). The new attempt records `retry_of` (the attempt the round rejected), starts with the step's `review.context` (`fresh` by default, a new conversation; `continue` keeps the author's), and holds, before any other note, one line naming the round and each verdict, for example `Review round 2 of 6 rejected the design step: need 2 of 2 with skeptic; got skeptic reject, builder approve. Address every reviewer's concern and say how.` (a reviewer whose verdict did not count reads `no counted verdict`), then each counted reviewer's write-up as a fenced block (below). The history line `review_retry` (`step`, `attempt`, `round`, `rounds`, `max_rounds`) records it. Its checks are judged as any attempt's, and once they pass its review is a new round, in a new directory (`<step>.<n>.r<round>`), in the window the rejected round kept (see [A review round's window](#a-review-rounds-window)). Once the step has spent its `max_rounds`, the run stops `review_rejected` (`limit: rounds`) and a plain `resume` is refused (`review_rounds_spent`); `workflow resume RUN --extra-rounds N` adds N rounds to that step (`review_rounds_extended` in the history, `steps.<step>.extra_rounds` in the run file) and runs the author again with the reviewers' write-ups, held to the attempt allowance as any retry is. `current.review.max_rounds` and the `max_rounds` of the reasons count the extra rounds.

A retry that waits for a lock its step's `uses:` names is still that retry when the wait ends: the run's `retry_of_pending` keeps, for that step, the attempt it retries, its `review.context` and its text, and `resume --from` drops it and takes its text out of the instructions the step named would get. Each retry is an attempt, so it counts against the run's and the step's `max_attempts`; review rounds count against neither, but against the token budget and the run's `timeout:` (the retry is held on `budget_spent` or `run_timed_out` as any attempt is, and a resume with `--extra-tokens` or `--extra-time` starts it as the retry it was). The run stops for a person with `review_rejected` instead of retrying when the run has spent its `max_attempts` (`details.limit` `run`), else the step its own (`step`; both with `attempts` and `max_attempts`), else the step its `max_rounds` (`rounds`). The retry the rejection would have started is kept as the run's `review_retry`: a resume with `--extra-attempts` starts it, `retry_of` the rejected attempt and told the verdicts and write-ups; `resume --from` drops it. A step's `review_rounds` counts every round started in the run, by any of its attempts, however the step was reached again: a rejection's retry, a resumed stopped round, a round refused as `review_tampered` or for a directory that could not be made (its number is used), a gate's `reject`, `resume --from` or an `on_fail` loop back to it, including rounds that approved an earlier attempt. Nothing resets it. A review the step reaches with every round spent starts no round: the step stays `reviewing`, the round held (`held: review_rounds_spent`), and the run stops with `review_rounds_spent`. A resume that would start another round, or run again a step whose last round rejected it, is refused with `review_rounds_spent`. At the cap only two things go on: `cancel`, which ends the run, and `resume --from STEP`, which runs the author again, after which the step's review stops `review_rounds_spent` once more, since nothing yet adds rounds to a step. Needs `workflow` 36 in `capabilities`.

#### An unchanged revision and missing verdicts

A rejected round records the revision it judged as `review_outcome.revision` (every decided round does). When the step's next round is about to be delivered and the step's last decided round rejected it, the engine first computes that round's revision ([A review round's revision](#a-review-rounds-revision)), with the run's lock given up; the same revision means the author's retry changed nothing the review reads, and the round is not delivered: the run stops for a person with `review_no_change` (`details`: `step`, `attempt`, `round`, `revision`, `rejected_round`, `rejected_attempt`; the history line `review_no_change` carries the same).
